The back of the book. Every rubric, prompt, hint, solution, and every variant that is meant to fail. Working a rung out unaided is the better path and most of the time it is worth the struggle. But reading the answer and reasoning backwards to why it is the answer is also how people learn, so it is here rather than withheld. Regenerate with npm run build:solutions.

Function

Core Functions 3 rubric 4 rungs 6 variants
  1. 3 Can state what a function has to do to earn the name: relate every input to exactly one output, and do nothing else you could observe.
  2. 3 Given a definition that falls short, can say which of the three requirements it breaks, rather than just that it is "impure".
  3. 2 Can write a definition with no input that falls through, including the cases a quick reading misses.

Rungs

Which one is a function in the strict sense?

choicerecognize

A function relates every input to exactly one output, and does nothing else that you could observe. Pick the one that meets all three.

demonstrates three-requirementsdiagnose
  1. wrong
    const half = (n) => {
  if (n % 2) return
  return n / 2
}

    Breaks the first promise. Odd numbers fall off the end and get undefined, so not every input has an output.

  2. wrong
    const roll = (sides) => Math.ceil(Math.random() * sides)

    Breaks the second. The same input gives different answers, so the input does not determine the output.

  3. correct
    const double = (n) => n * 2

    All three hold. Every number has a double, it is always the same one, and nothing outside is touched.

  4. wrong
    const track = (n) => {
  hits.push(n)
  return n * 2
}

    Breaks the third. The return value is fine, but pushing to hits is visible from outside.

Which promise does each one break?

choicerecognize

Each definition below fails exactly one of the three. Select the ones that fail the second: same input, same output.

demonstrates diagnose
  1. correct
    const idFor = (name) => `${name}-${Date.now()}`

    Reads the clock, so calling it twice with the same name gives two answers.

  2. wrong
    const first = (xs) => xs[0]

    Deterministic and effect-free. It fails the first promise instead: an empty list has no first element.

  3. correct
    let count = 0
const next = () => ++count

    Depends on a binding outside itself, so the answer changes every call. It also fails the third by writing to it.

  4. wrong
    const save = (user) => {
  db.write(user)
  return user.id
}

    Fails the third. The return value is perfectly predictable; the write to db is the problem.

  5. correct
    const rateFor = (amount) => amount * TAX_RATE

    TAX_RATE is not an input. Change it and the same amount gives a different answer.

Relate every input to exactly one output

codeimplement

toLabel should hand back a string for any integer at all, with no gaps. Negative, zero, and huge values included.

demonstrates no-gapsthree-requirements
2 hints
  1. Every branch has to end in a return. A path that falls off the end returns undefined, which is a gap.
  2. Think about what is left over once you have handled negative and zero.
Solution
// toLabel :: Number -> String
const toLabel = (n) => {
  if (n < 0) return 'below'
  if (n === 0) return 'zero'
  return 'above'
}
Starter · 1 export: toLabel
// toLabel :: Number -> String
// negative -> "below", zero -> "zero", positive -> "above"
const toLabel = (n) => {
  if (n < 0) return 'below'
  if (n === 0) return 'zero'
}
2 variants that must fail
const toLabel = (n) => {
  if (n < 0) return 'below'
  if (n === 0) return 'zero'
}
const toLabel = (n) => {
  if (n <= 0) return 'below'
  return 'above'
}

Repair three near-misses

codeapply

Each of these fails exactly one of the three requirements. Fix each one so it relates every input to exactly one output and does nothing else observable, without changing what it is obviously for.

demonstrates diagnoseno-gapsthree-requirements
3 hints
  1. half is fine for even numbers and falls off a cliff for odd ones. Every input needs an output.
  2. pick gives a different answer each time it is called with the same list.
  3. stamp reaches outside itself for the time. Anything it needs, it should be handed.
Solution
// half :: Number -> Number
const half = (n) => n / 2

// pick :: [a] -> a
const pick = (xs) => xs[0]

// stamp :: (Number, String) -> String
const stamp = (at, message) => at + ' ' + message
Starter · 3 exports: half, pick, stamp
// half :: Number -> Number
const half = (n) => {
  if (n % 2 === 0) return n / 2
}

// pick :: [a] -> a
const pick = (xs) => xs[Math.floor(Math.random() * xs.length)]

// stamp :: String -> String
const stamp = (message) => Date.now() + ' ' + message

  if (n % 2 === 0) return n / 2
}

const stamp = (at, message) => at + ' ' + message

const stamp = (at, message) => Date.now() + ' ' + message
const half = (n) => (n % 2 === 0 ? n / 2 : null)

const stamp = (at, message) => at + ' ' + message

Arity

Core Functions 3 rubric 3 rungs 5 variants
  1. 1 Can name the arity of any definition, from nullary through n-ary, without reaching for the docs.
  2. 3 Can predict what fn.length reports for a definition using defaults, rest, or destructuring, and knows it is the declared arity rather than the call arity.
  3. 1 Can say what breaks when machinery reads fn.length and gets an answer it did not expect, as auto-currying does.

Rungs

What does fn.length report?

exprrecognize

Every one of these declares its parameters differently. Write the array of what fn.length gives for each, in order.

demonstrates declared-vs-call
2 hints
  1. Counting stops at the first parameter with a default. A rest parameter is never counted.
  2. A destructured parameter is still one parameter.
Given
const a = (x, y) => 0
const b = (x, y, z = 0) => 0
const c = (x = 1, y) => 0
const d = (x, ...rest) => 0
const e = ({ x, y }) => 0
Answer
[a.length, b.length, c.length, d.length, e.length]

evaluates to [2,2,0,1,1]

Rejected
  • [2, 3, 2, 2, 2]
  • [2, 2, 0, 2, 1]
  • [2, 2, 0, 1, 2]

Name an arity

codeimplement

Write arityName so it turns a function into the word for how many arguments it declares. Anything above three is n-ary.

demonstrates name-itdeclared-vs-call
2 hints
  1. The words go nullary, unary, binary, ternary.
  2. fn.length gives you the number. Everything above 3 shares one name.
Solution
// arityName :: Function -> String
const arityName = (fn) => {
  const names = ['nullary', 'unary', 'binary', 'ternary']
  return names[fn.length] || 'n-ary'
}
Starter · 1 export: arityName
// arityName :: Function -> String
const arityName = (fn) => {
  // nullary, unary, binary, ternary, or n-ary
}
2 variants that must fail
const arityName = (fn) => {
  const names = ['unary', 'binary', 'ternary']
  return names[fn.length] || 'n-ary'
}
const arityName = (fn) => {
  const names = ['nullary', 'unary', 'binary', 'ternary']
  return names[fn.length]
}

Predict where currying goes wrong

codeapply

curry waits for fn.length arguments before calling through. Write curriesBadly, which reports whether a function is one it will mishandle.

demonstrates why-it-mattersdeclared-vs-call
2 hints
  1. curry compares the arguments it has collected against fn.length.
  2. A function whose declared arity is smaller than the number of arguments it really wants gets called too early.
Solution
const curry = (fn) => {
  const collect = (...args) =>
    args.length >= fn.length ? fn(...args) : (...more) => collect(...args, ...more)
  return collect
}

// curriesBadly :: (Function, Number) -> Boolean
const curriesBadly = (fn, wanted) => fn.length < wanted
Starter · 1 export: curriesBadly
// curry calls through once it has collected fn.length arguments.
const curry = (fn) => {
  const collect = (...args) =>
    args.length >= fn.length ? fn(...args) : (...more) => collect(...args, ...more)
  return collect
}

// curriesBadly :: (Function, Number) -> Boolean
// wanted is how many arguments the function actually needs to do its job.
const curriesBadly = (fn, wanted) => {
}
3 variants that must fail
const curriesBadly = (fn, wanted) => fn.length > wanted
const curriesBadly = (fn, wanted) => fn.length === 0
const curriesBadly = (fn, wanted) => fn.length !== wanted

Lambda

Core Functions 3 rubric 3 rungs 5 variants
  1. 2 Can say what "a function is a value" buys you: it can be passed, returned, stored, and applied on the spot, with no name involved.
  2. 1 Knows a name adds nothing semantically, so can decide when naming a callback helps a reader and when it just adds a hop.
  3. 2 Can rewrite a pipeline of single-use named helpers as inline lambdas without changing what it does.

Rungs

What makes something a lambda?

choicerecognize

A lambda is a function treated as a value: it needs no name, and it can be passed, returned, and stored. Select every true statement.

demonstrates function-as-value
  1. correct
    // A lambda can be passed straight to another function
[1, 2, 3].map(x => x * 2)

    Being usable as an argument without ever being named is the whole point.

  2. wrong
    // A lambda must be assigned to a variable first

    You can name one for convenience, but it is a value either way and can be used inline.

  3. correct
    // A lambda can be returned from another function
const adder = a => b => a + b

    Returning one is as ordinary as returning a number.

  4. correct
    // A lambda can be invoked the moment it is defined
(x => x * 2)(4)

    It is a value, so it can be applied on the spot. That is the immediately invoked form.

  5. wrong
    // Lambdas are a different kind of thing from named functions

    Same thing. The only difference is that one happens to have a name bound to it.

Inline the named callbacks

codeguided

This pipeline names three one-line helpers that are used once each. Replace them with lambdas written where they are used.

demonstrates inline-itname-is-a-choice
2 hints
  1. Each helper body becomes the body of an arrow passed straight to map or filter.
  2. process should end up as one chained expression with no helper names left.
Solution
// process :: [Number] -> [String]
const process = (ns) =>
  ns
    .map((n) => n * 2)
    .filter((n) => n > 10)
    .map((n) => `#${n}`)
Starter · 1 export: process
function double(n) { return n * 2 }
function isBig(n) { return n > 10 }
function label(n) { return `#${n}` }

// process :: [Number] -> [String]
const process = (ns) => ns.map(double).filter(isBig).map(label)
2 variants that must fail

    .map((n) => `#${n}`)

Pass functions around without naming them

codeapply

A lambda is a function used as a value. Build pipeline from a list of inline lambdas, and twice, which takes a function and applies it two times.

demonstrates function-as-valueinline-it
3 hints
  1. twice receives a function as an argument. Call it, then call it again on the result.
  2. steps is an array whose elements are functions. Nothing stops a function being an array element.
  3. pipeline runs the steps left to right over a starting value. reduce already has that shape.
Solution
// twice :: (a -> a) -> a -> a
const twice = (f) => (x) => f(f(x))

// steps :: [Number -> Number]   add one, then double, then subtract three
const steps = [(n) => n + 1, (n) => n * 2, (n) => n - 3]

// pipeline :: ([a -> a], a) -> a
const pipeline = (fns, x) => fns.reduce((acc, f) => f(acc), x)
Starter · 3 exports: twice, steps, pipeline

const pipeline = (fns, x) => x

const twice = (f) => (x) => f(f(x))
const steps = [(n) => n - 3, (n) => n * 2, (n) => n + 1]
const pipeline = (fns, x) => fns.reduce((acc, f) => f(acc), x)

Higher-Order Functions (HOF)

Core Functions 4 rubric 2 rungs 5 variants
  1. 1 Can recognize both shapes: a function that takes a function, and one that returns a function.
  2. 1 Can write one that takes a function, walking a structure and leaving the input alone.
  3. 2 Can write one that returns a function, and knows the closure is what makes the returned function remember anything.
  4. 1 Can wrap an existing function to change when or how often it runs, without changing what it computes.

Rungs

Write filter and is

codeimplement

A higher-order function takes a function, returns one, or both. filter takes one; is returns one.

demonstrates two-shapestakes-onereturns-one
2 hints
  1. filter walks the list and keeps the elements the predicate says yes to.
  2. is does not test anything itself. It hands back a function that does.
Solution
// filter :: (a -> Boolean) -> [a] -> [a]
const filter = (predicate, xs) => {
  const out = []
  for (const x of xs) {
    if (predicate(x)) out.push(x)
  }
  return out
}

// is :: Type -> a -> Boolean
const is = (type) => (x) => x instanceof type
Starter · 2 exports: filter, is
// filter :: (a -> Boolean) -> [a] -> [a]
const filter = (predicate, xs) => {
  // keep the elements the predicate accepts
}

// is :: Type -> a -> Boolean
const is = (type) => {
  // return a function that tests its argument against type
}
3 variants that must fail
const filter = (predicate, xs) => {
  for (let i = xs.length - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
    if (!predicate(xs[i])) xs.splice(i, 1)
  }
  return xs
}
const is = (type) => (x) => x instanceof type
const filter = (predicate, xs) => xs.reduce((acc, x) => (predicate(x) ? [...acc, x] : acc), [])
const is = (type, x) => x instanceof type
const filter = (predicate, xs) => xs.reduce((acc, x) => (predicate(x) ? acc : [...acc, x]), [])
const is = (type) => (x) => x instanceof type

Write once

codeapply

once wraps a function so it can only ever run a single time. Every later call gives back the first result without calling through.

demonstrates wrappingreturns-one
2 hints
  1. You need to remember two things between calls: whether it has run, and what it gave back.
  2. A closure is the place to keep them.
Solution
// once :: (a -> b) -> (a -> b)
const once = (fn) => {
  let called = false
  let result
  return (...args) => {
    if (!called) {
      called = true
      result = fn(...args)
    }
    return result
  }
}
Starter · 1 export: once
// once :: (a -> b) -> (a -> b)
const once = (fn) => {
  // call fn the first time, then keep handing back that same result
}
2 variants that must fail
const once = (fn) => {
  let result
  return (...args) => {
    if (!result) result = fn(...args)
    return result
  }
}
const once = (fn) => (...args) => fn(...args)

Closure

Core Functions 4 rubric 3 rungs 4 variants
  1. 2 Knows a closure captures the binding, not a copy of the value, so it sees later changes to that variable.
  2. 1 Can use a closure to give something state that nothing outside can reach or corrupt.
  3. 1 Knows each call to the enclosing function makes fresh bindings, so two instances do not share state.
  4. 2 Can diagnose and fix the classic loop-capture bug, and can say why let fixes it and var does not.

Rungs

A counter with private state

codeimplement

A closure keeps the variables its function was created with alive. Use one to give each counter its own count that nothing outside can reach.

demonstrates private-stateindependent-instances
2 hints
  1. Declare the count inside makeCounter, not outside it.
  2. Each call to makeCounter runs the body again, which creates a fresh variable for that counter to close over.
Solution
// makeCounter :: () -> (() -> Number)
const makeCounter = () => {
  let count = 0
  return () => {
    count += 1
    return count
  }
}
Starter · 1 export: makeCounter
// makeCounter :: () -> (() -> Number)
const makeCounter = () => {
  // return a function that hands back 1, then 2, then 3...
}
2 variants that must fail
let count = 0
const makeCounter = () => () => {
  count += 1
  return count
}
const makeCounter = () => {
  let count = 0
  return () => count + 1
}

Predicate

Core Functions 3 rubric 2 rungs 4 variants
  1. 1 Knows a predicate answers true or false about one value, and that answering anything else breaks the contract.
  2. 2 Can write combinators that build a bigger predicate from smaller ones, and knows they return predicates so they keep composing.
  3. 1 Can turn a rule stated in English into a composition of named predicates, with the grouping right.

Rungs

Combine predicates

codeimplement

A predicate is a function that answers true or false. Write the three combinators that build bigger predicates out of smaller ones.

demonstrates what-it-iscombinators
2 hints
  1. Each one returns a new predicate, so the shape is (...preds) => (x) => ....
  2. both is an and, either is an or, not flips the answer.
Solution
// both :: (a -> Boolean) -> (a -> Boolean) -> (a -> Boolean)
const both = (f, g) => (x) => f(x) && g(x)

// either :: (a -> Boolean) -> (a -> Boolean) -> (a -> Boolean)
const either = (f, g) => (x) => f(x) || g(x)

// not :: (a -> Boolean) -> (a -> Boolean)
const not = (f) => (x) => !f(x)
Starter · 3 exports: both, either, not

const both = (f, g) => {
}

// either :: (a -> Boolean) -> (a -> Boolean) -> (a -> Boolean)
const either = (f, g) => {
}

// not :: (a -> Boolean) -> (a -> Boolean)
const not = (f) => {
}
2 variants that must fail

const either = (f, g) => (x) => f(x) && g(x)

const both = (f, g) => (x) => f(x) & g(x)
const either = (f, g) => (x) => f(x) | g(x)
const not = (f) => (x) => !f(x)

// wanted :: Item -> Boolean

Pure Function

Core Functions 4 rubric 3 rungs 7 variants
  1. 2 Can state the two requirements separately: the output depends on the inputs alone, and nothing observable changes.
  2. 2 Given an impure function, can say which of the two it breaks, and name the specific line that breaks it.
  3. 2 Can purify a function by taking what it read as an argument and returning a new value instead of editing the one it was given.
  4. 2 Knows that copying the top level of a structure is not enough, and can spot a copy that still shares a nested reference.

Rungs

Which one is pure?

choicerecognize

A pure function depends only on its inputs and changes nothing outside itself. Pick the pure one.

demonstrates two-requirementsspot-the-breach
  1. wrong
    let greeting
const greet = (name) => {
  greeting = `Hi, ${name}`
}

    Writes to greeting, which lives outside the function.

  2. wrong
    const greet = () => `Hi, ${window.name}`

    Reads window.name, so the same call can return different results.

  3. correct
    const greet = (name) => `Hi, ${name}`

    The output is determined by name alone and nothing else is touched.

  4. wrong
    const greet = (name) => {
  console.log(name)
  return `Hi, ${name}`
}

    Logging is a side effect, even though the return value is predictable.

Purify addItem

codeimplement

This addItem mutates the cart and reads the clock. Make it pure: return a new cart, leave the input alone, and take the timestamp as a third argument.

demonstrates purifyshallow-copy-traptwo-requirements
3 hints
  1. Spread the old cart into a new object rather than writing into it.
  2. [...cart.items, item] builds a new list. cart.items.push(item) changes the old one.
  3. The now parameter is there so the function never has to ask the clock itself.
Solution
// addItem :: (Cart, Item, Number) -> Cart
const addItem = (cart, item, now) => ({
  ...cart,
  items: [...cart.items, item],
  updatedAt: now
})
Starter · 1 export: addItem
// addItem :: (Cart, Item, Number) -> Cart
const addItem = (cart, item, now) => {
  cart.items.push(item)
  cart.updatedAt = Date.now()
  return cart
}
3 variants that must fail
const addItem = (cart, item, now) => {

Thunk

Core Functions 3 rubric 2 rungs 4 variants
  1. 1 Knows a thunk is a zero-argument function standing in for a value that has not been computed yet.
  2. 2 Can wrap work so that nothing runs until the thunk is called, and can prove it by counting calls.
  3. 1 Knows a plain thunk recomputes on every call, and can add caching without breaking on a falsy or undefined result.

Rungs

Defer the work

codeimplement

A thunk is a function of no arguments that stands in for a value not computed yet. Write delay so nothing runs until the thunk is called.

demonstrates what-it-isdefer
2 hints
  1. delay should not call fn itself. It should hand back something that will.
  2. The returned function takes no arguments.
Solution
// delay :: (() -> a) -> (() -> a)
const delay = (fn) => () => fn()

  // hand back a thunk that runs fn only when it is called
}

  const value = fn()

Compute it at most once

codeapply

lazy is a thunk that remembers. The work is skipped until the first call, and never repeated after it.

demonstrates recompute-vs-cachedefer
2 hints
  1. Keep a flag for whether it has run, separate from the value.
  2. A value of undefined or 0 is still a computed value, so do not use the value itself as the flag.
Solution
// lazy :: (() -> a) -> (() -> a)
const lazy = (fn) => {
  let forced = false
  let value
  return () => {
    if (!forced) {
      forced = true
      value = fn()
    }
    return value
  }
}
Starter · 1 export: lazy
// lazy :: (() -> a) -> (() -> a)
const lazy = (fn) => {
  // run fn at most once, then hand back the same value forever
}
2 variants that must fail

  let value
  return () => {
    if (value === undefined) value = fn()
    return value
  }
}
const lazy = (fn) => {
  const value = fn()
  return () => value
}

Partial function

Core Functions 3 rubric 3 rungs 7 variants
  1. 3 Can name the three ways a function can be undefined somewhere: it throws, it returns nothing useful, or it never returns at all.
  2. 2 Can look at a signature and say where reality will not match it, including the quiet cases that return undefined rather than throwing.
  3. 2 Can probe a function across a set of inputs and collect the ones it is not defined for, without the survey dying on the first throw.

Rungs

Which of these are partial?

choicerecognize

A partial function is not defined for every input in its domain: some inputs throw, hang, or hand back nothing useful. Select every partial one.

demonstrates three-failuresspot-them
  1. correct
    // first :: [a] -> a
const first = (xs) => xs[0]

    An empty list has no first element, so it quietly returns undefined instead of an a.

  2. wrong
    // length :: [a] -> Number
const length = (xs) => xs.length

    Every list has a length, the empty one included.

  3. correct
    // parse :: String -> Object
const parse = (s) => JSON.parse(s)

    Most strings are not JSON, and those throw rather than return an Object.

  4. wrong
    // half :: Number -> Number
const half = (n) => n / 2

    Every number halves. Even the awkward ones give a number back.

  5. correct
    // countDown :: Number -> [Number]
const countDown = (n) =>
  n === 0 ? [0] : [n, ...countDown(n - 1)]

    A negative n never reaches the base case, so it recurses until the stack runs out.

Find the inputs that break it

codeimplement

Write breaksOn, which runs a function against each input and collects the ones it is not defined for: the inputs that throw, or that give back undefined.

demonstrates surveythree-failures
2 hints
  1. Wrap the call in try/catch. A throw counts as undefined behavior, and so does a return of undefined.
  2. Collect the inputs themselves, not the results.
Solution
// breaksOn :: (a -> b) -> [a] -> [a]
const breaksOn = (fn, inputs) =>
  inputs.filter((x) => {
    try {
      return fn(x) === undefined
    } catch (e) {
      return true
    }
  })
Starter · 1 export: breaksOn
// breaksOn :: (a -> b) -> [a] -> [a]

  // keep the inputs fn is not defined for
}
3 variants that must fail

  inputs.filter((x) => {
    try {
      fn(x)
      return false
    } catch (e) {
      return true
    }
  })

  inputs.map((x) => {
    try {
      return fn(x)
    } catch (e) {
      return x
    }
  })

Survey three functions, then make them total

codeapply

probe runs a function over a list of inputs and collects the ones it is not defined for, without dying on the first throw. Then make safeHead, safeDiv and safeParse total by widening what they return.

demonstrates spot-themsurveythree-failures
3 hints
  1. probe has to survive a throw, so each call needs its own try/catch. Collect the input, not the error.
  2. A value that is undefined, NaN, or a throw all count as not defined for that input.
  3. Widening means every input gets an answer. null for the missing case is enough here, as long as nothing throws and nothing comes back undefined or NaN.
Solution
// probe :: ((a -> b), [a]) -> [a]   the inputs it is not defined for
const probe = (fn, inputs) =>
  inputs.filter((x) => {
    try {
      const r = fn(x)
      return r === undefined || (typeof r === 'number' && Number.isNaN(r))
    } catch {
      return true
    }
  })

const safeDiv = (a, b) => (b === 0 ? null : a / b)

const safeParse = (s) => (Number.isNaN(Number(s)) ? null : Number(s))

Total Function

Core Functions 3 rubric 2 rungs 3 variants
  1. 1 Knows total means an answer for every input in the domain, and that not throwing is necessary but not sufficient.
  2. 1 Can make a function total by widening what it returns, rather than by inventing a value it does not have.
  3. 2 Knows the empty case usually has a right answer already, and can say what it is for a sum, a product, and a repeat.

Rungs

Make three functions total

codeimplement

Each of these is undefined somewhere. Give every input an answer: sum of an empty list, first of an empty list, and times with a negative count.

demonstrates widen-the-outputidentity-for-empty
3 hints
  1. An empty sum is 0, because adding nothing changes nothing.
  2. first cannot invent an element, so widen what it returns instead: { found: false } when there is none.
  3. A negative repeat count is the same as repeating zero times.
Solution
// sum :: [Number] -> Number
const sum = (xs) => xs.reduce((a, b) => a + b, 0)

// first :: [a] -> { found :: Boolean, value :: a }
const first = (xs) => (xs.length > 0 ? { found: true, value: xs[0] } : { found: false })

// times :: Number -> String -> String
const times = (n, s) => (n > 0 ? s.repeat(n) : '')

What should the empty case give?

Each of these folds has a right answer for the empty list, and it is never an error. Write them as an array, in order.

demonstrates identity-for-emptydefinition
2 hints
  1. The answer is whatever leaves the other operand alone.
  2. Adding it changes nothing, multiplying by it changes nothing, joining it changes nothing.

Trampoline

Core Functions 3 rubric 3 rungs 6 variants
  1. 2 Can say why deep self-recursion exhausts the stack in JavaScript, and why writing it in tail position does not help.
  2. 2 Can rewrite a recursive function to return a thunk for the next step instead of calling it.
  3. 2 Can write the loop that keeps invoking thunks until a real value falls out, and knows one unwrapping is not enough.

Rungs

Bounce instead of recursing

codeimplement

A trampoline turns deep recursion into a loop. sumBelow should return a thunk instead of calling itself, and trampoline should keep calling thunks until a real value falls out.

demonstrates return-instead-of-callthe-driver
2 hints
  1. trampoline is a while loop: while the result is a function, call it.
  2. sumBelow never calls itself directly. It returns () => sumBelow(...) and lets the trampoline do the calling.
Solution
// trampoline :: (a | (() -> a)) -> a
const trampoline = (result) => {
  while (typeof result === 'function') {
    result = result()
  }
  return result
}

// sumBelow :: (Number, Number) -> Number | (() -> ...)
const sumBelow = (n, acc = 0) => {
  if (n <= 0) return acc
  return () => sumBelow(n - 1, acc + n)
}
Starter · 2 exports: trampoline, sumBelow
// trampoline :: (a | (() -> a)) -> a
const trampoline = (result) => {
  // keep calling while you are handed a function
}

// sumBelow :: (Number, Number) -> Number | (() -> ...)
const sumBelow = (n, acc = 0) => {
  if (n <= 0) return acc
  return sumBelow(n - 1, acc + n)
}
3 variants that must fail

  while (typeof result === 'function') result = result()
  return result
}

  if (n <= 0) return acc
  return sumBelow(n - 1, acc + n)
}

  if (n <= 0) return acc
  return () => sumBelow(n - 1, acc + n)
}

  while (typeof result === 'function') result = result()
  return result

  if (n <= 0) return acc

Why does a trampoline help?

choicerecognize

Pick the reason it survives a million steps when plain recursion does not.

demonstrates why-the-stack-runs-out
  1. correct
    // Each step returns to the loop before the next one starts,
// so only one frame is ever on the stack

    The recursion is turned inside out: the loop drives it, and no frame waits on another.

  2. wrong
    // It makes each step faster

    It is usually a little slower. What it buys is a constant stack, not speed.

  3. wrong
    // JavaScript engines optimize tail calls when you return a function

    Most engines do not implement tail call elimination at all. The trampoline exists precisely because they do not.

  4. wrong
    // It runs the steps in parallel

    Strictly one at a time, in a loop, on one thread.

Show the stack running out, then survive it

codebreak

countDirect recurses the ordinary way and countBounced returns a thunk for each step. Write both, plus the driver. You pass when the direct one blows the stack at 100000 and the bounced one does not.

demonstrates why-the-stack-runs-outreturn-instead-of-callthe-driver
3 hints
  1. countDirect is the naive version. Do not try to make it survive; the rung is about watching it fail.
  2. countBounced returns () => countBounced(...) instead of calling itself, so the frame is gone before the next step starts.
  3. run keeps calling while it is holding a function. One unwrapping is not enough, because the thunk returns another thunk.
Solution
// countDirect :: (Number, Number) -> Number   plain recursion, will not survive
const countDirect = (n, acc = 0) => (n === 0 ? acc : countDirect(n - 1, acc + n))

// countBounced :: (Number, Number) -> Number | (() -> ...)
const countBounced = (n, acc = 0) => (n === 0 ? acc : () => countBounced(n - 1, acc + n))

// run :: (a | (() -> a)) -> a
const run = (result) => {
  let r = result
  while (typeof r === 'function') r = r()
  return r
}

// countBounced :: (Number, Number) -> Number | (() -> ...)

// run :: (a | (() -> a)) -> a

Currying

Composition & Flow 3 rubric 3 rungs 4 variants
  1. 2 Can recognize a fully curried definition, and knows every step takes exactly one argument.
  2. 1 Can curry a two-argument function by hand, and knows nothing should run until the last argument arrives.
  3. 1 Can say why currying makes point-free style possible, and can use it to drop a named argument.

Rungs

Spot the curried functions

choicerecognize

A curried function takes one argument at a time. Select every definition below that is fully curried.

demonstrates one-at-a-time
  1. wrong
    const add = (a, b) => a + b

    Takes both arguments at once, so its arity is 2.

  2. correct
    const add = a => b => a + b

    Each call takes one argument and returns the next function.

  3. correct
    const clamp = min => max => x =>
  Math.min(max, Math.max(min, x))

    Three unary steps. Curried functions can be as deep as you need.

  4. wrong
    const sum3 = a => (b, c) => a + b + c

    The second step takes two arguments, so it is only partly curried.

  5. wrong
    const sumAll = (...xs) =>
  xs.reduce((a, b) => a + b, 0)

    Variadic: it takes any number of arguments in one call.

Write curry2

codeimplement

Turn any two-argument function into a curried one. Nothing should run until both arguments have arrived.

demonstrates write-itone-at-a-time
2 hints
  1. The outer function takes f and returns a function that takes a.
  2. That function returns another function, and only the innermost one has both a and b to call f with.
Solution
// curry2 :: ((a, b) -> c) -> a -> b -> c
const curry2 = (f) => (a) => (b) => f(a, b)
Starter · 1 export: curry2
// curry2 :: ((a, b) -> c) -> a -> b -> c
const curry2 = (f) => {
  // return a function that takes a,
  // which returns a function that takes b
}
2 variants that must fail
const curry2 = (f) => (a, b) => f(a, b)
const curry2 = (f) => (a) => f(a, undefined)

Auto Currying

Composition & Flow 3 rubric 3 rungs 3 variants
  1. 1 Can write a curry that accepts any grouping of arguments, not just one at a time.
  2. 2 Knows it decides when to call through by reading fn.length, and can name the definitions where that goes wrong.
  3. 2 Knows nothing runs until enough arguments have arrived, however they were grouped.

Rungs

Curry any function automatically

codeimplement

Hand-curried functions only accept one argument at a time. curry should accept any grouping: f(1)(2)(3), f(1, 2)(3), and f(1, 2, 3) all work.

demonstrates any-groupingwaits
2 hints
  1. fn.length tells you how many arguments are still expected in total.
  2. When you have collected enough, call through. Otherwise hand back a function that keeps collecting.
Solution
// curry :: ((a, b, ...) -> r) -> a -> b -> ... -> r
const curry = (fn) => {
  const collect = (...args) =>
    args.length >= fn.length ? fn(...args) : (...more) => collect(...args, ...more)
  return collect
}
Starter · 1 export: curry
// curry :: ((a, b, ...) -> r) -> a -> b -> ... -> r
const curry = (fn) => {
  // collect arguments until you have fn.length of them
}
3 variants that must fail
const curry = (fn) => {

    args.length >= fn.length ? fn(...args) : (x) => collect([...args, x])

  const collect = (...args) =>

Where does auto-currying stop working?

choicerecognize

Auto-currying leans on fn.length. Pick the function it cannot curry correctly.

demonstrates reads-length
  1. wrong
    const add = (a, b, c) => a + b + c

    length is 3, which is exactly right.

  2. correct
    const sum = (...ns) => ns.reduce((a, b) => a + b, 0)

    A rest parameter makes length 0, so curry calls through immediately and never collects anything.

  3. wrong
    const greet = (greeting, name) => `${greeting}, ${name}`

    length is 2. Nothing unusual here.

  4. wrong
    const pair = (a) => (b) => [a, b]

    length is 1 and it is already curried, so currying it again changes nothing.

Predict what arity each definition reports

exprrecognize

Auto-currying decides when to call through by reading fn.length. Type an array of the four lengths, in order. Getting this right is the difference between using it and being surprised by it.

demonstrates reads-lengthwaits
3 hints
  1. fn.length counts the parameters before the first one with a default, and stops there.
  2. A rest parameter is not counted at all.
  3. Destructuring one object still counts as one parameter.
Given
const plain   = (a, b, c) => a + b + c
const defaulted = (a, b, c = 1) => a + b + c
const rested  = (a, ...rest) => a
const destructured = ({ x, y }) => x + y
Answer
[plain.length, defaulted.length, rested.length, destructured.length]

evaluates to [3,2,1,1]

Rejected
  • [3, 3, 2, 2]
  • [3, 3, 1, 1]
  • [3, 2, 2, 1]
  • [3, 2, 1, 2]

Function Composition

Composition & Flow 3 rubric 2 rungs 5 variants
  1. 1 Can write variadic compose and pipe, and knows which way each one reads.
  2. 1 Knows composition is associative and that identity is neutral, and can say what those buy you.
  3. 1 Can express a multi-step transformation as one composed function, in the order the steps happen.

Rungs

Write compose and pipe

codeimplement

Both take any number of functions and thread a value through them. compose reads right to left, the way the maths does; pipe reads left to right.

demonstrates both-directionslaws
2 hints
  1. reduceRight walks a list from the end, which is the direction compose needs.
  2. They are the same function with the list reversed.
Solution
// compose :: ((y -> z), (x -> y), ...) -> a -> z
const compose = (...fns) => (x) => fns.reduceRight((acc, fn) => fn(acc), x)

// pipe :: ((a -> x), (x -> y), ...) -> a -> z
const pipe = (...fns) => (x) => fns.reduce((acc, fn) => fn(acc), x)

  // apply the leftmost function first

Point-Free Style

Composition & Flow 3 rubric 3 rungs 6 variants
  1. 3 Can tell a point-free definition from one that names an argument only to pass it straight on.
  2. 2 Can rewrite a definition to drop the argument it never really used, using composition or a curried helper.
  3. 2 Knows point-free is a readability choice, not a virtue, and can say when naming the argument is clearer.

Rungs

Which definitions are point-free?

choicerecognize

Point-free means the definition never names the data it operates on. Select every definition that manages it.

demonstrates spot-itwhen-not-to
  1. correct
    const inc = map(add(1))

    No parameter is introduced. It is built from other functions.

  2. wrong
    const inc = (xs) => map(add(1))(xs)

    It names xs only to pass it straight through, which is the argument point-free style removes.

  3. correct
    const total = reduce(add, 0)

    Two arguments are fixed and the data is never mentioned.

  4. correct
    const shout = compose(exclaim, upper)

    A composition of two functions, and no parameter anywhere.

  5. wrong
    const pairUp = (a) => (b) => [a, b]

    It introduces two parameters. Naming arguments is fine, it is just not point-free.

Functional Combinator

Composition & Flow 3 rubric 3 rungs 7 variants
  1. 2 Knows a combinator uses nothing but its own arguments, and can tell one from a function that closes over something.
  2. 2 Can write I, K, C and S, and say what each does in one sentence.
  3. 2 Can verify a combinator identity such as S(K)(K) behaving like I, and knows a failure points at one of the definitions.

Rungs

The classic combinators

codeimplement

A combinator is a function built only from its own arguments, with nothing free. Write I (identity), K (constant), C (flip), and S (substitution).

demonstrates the-classicsidentities
3 hints
  1. I hands its argument straight back. K ignores its second argument entirely.
  2. C takes a two-step function and swaps the order the arguments arrive in.
  3. S is f => g => x => f(x)(g(x)): both branches see the same x.
Solution
// I :: a -> a
const I = (x) => x

// K :: a -> b -> a
const K = (x) => (y) => x

// C :: (a -> b -> c) -> b -> a -> c
const C = (f) => (b) => (a) => f(a)(b)

// S :: (a -> b -> c) -> (a -> b) -> a -> c
const S = (f) => (g) => (x) => f(x)(g(x))

What disqualifies a combinator?

choicerecognize

A combinator uses nothing but its own arguments. Pick the one that is not a combinator.

demonstrates no-free-variables
  1. wrong
    const I = (x) => x

    Only its own argument. The simplest combinator there is.

  2. wrong
    const B = (f) => (g) => (x) => f(g(x))

    Three arguments, nothing free. This is composition.

  3. correct
    const T = (x) => x * TAX_RATE

    TAX_RATE comes from outside. A free variable is exactly what a combinator does not have.

  4. wrong
    const W = (f) => (x) => f(x)(x)

    Uses its argument twice, which is fine. Nothing comes from outside.

Continuation

Composition & Flow 3 rubric 3 rungs 7 variants
  1. 2 Can identify the continuation in a call: the function that says what happens once the answer exists.
  2. 2 Can convert a direct-style function to continuation-passing style, replacing every return with a call.
  3. 1 Can chain two continuation-passing steps so the first feeds the second, calling the outer continuation exactly once.

Rungs

Convert to continuation-passing style

codeimplement

In continuation-passing style a function never returns. It takes an extra argument, the continuation, and hands its answer to that instead.

demonstrates convertsequence
2 hints
  1. Replace every return x with done(x).
  2. When one step feeds another, the first step passes a continuation that does the rest.
Solution
// addCps :: (Number, Number, (Number -> r)) -> r
const addCps = (a, b, done) => done(a + b)

// squareCps :: (Number, (Number -> r)) -> r
const squareCps = (n, done) => done(n * n)

// addThenSquare :: (Number, Number, (Number -> r)) -> r
const addThenSquare = (a, b, done) =>
  addCps(a, b, (sum) => squareCps(sum, done))

What is the continuation?

choicerecognize

In addCps(2, 3, (sum) => console.log(sum)), which part is the continuation?

demonstrates what-it-is
  1. correct
    (sum) => console.log(sum)

    It is "the rest of the program": what should happen once the answer exists.

  2. wrong
    addCps

    That is the function taking a continuation, not the continuation itself.

  3. wrong
    2 and 3

    Ordinary arguments.

  4. wrong
    The value 5 that addCps produces

    That is what gets handed to the continuation, not the continuation.

Write the continuation down, then intercept one

codeapply

In square(add(1, n)) + 10, the continuation of the add call is everything still waiting on its answer. Write that as a function. Then write intercept, which wraps a continuation-passing function so you can watch what flows through it.

demonstrates what-it-isconvert
3 hints
  1. Ask what happens after add produces its answer. Squaring, then adding ten. That is the continuation.
  2. The continuation takes the answer as its argument, so it starts (x) =>.
  3. intercept gets a continuation-passing function and a spy. It hands the function a continuation of its own making, which calls the spy and then the real one.
Solution
const square = (x) => x * x
// the whole program, for reference
const program = (n) => square(add(1, n)) + 10

// restOfProgram :: Number -> Number
// everything still waiting on the answer to add(1, n)
const restOfProgram = (x) => square(x) + 10

// intercept :: (((a, (b -> c)) -> c), (b -> void)) -> ((a, (b -> c)) -> c)
const intercept = (cpsFn, spy) => (value, k) =>
  cpsFn(value, (answer) => {
    spy(answer)
    return k(answer)
  })

Lazy evaluation

Composition & Flow 3 rubric 2 rungs 4 variants
  1. 1 Knows nothing is computed until something asks, and can build an endless source that costs nothing to define.
  2. 2 Can take exactly as many values as needed, and knows why pulling one extra breaks against an endless source.
  3. 1 Can filter an endless source without materializing it, stopping as soon as enough has been collected.

Rungs

An infinite list you can take from

codeimplement

Lazy evaluation puts off work until the answer is needed. Write an endless naturals and a take that pulls only as many as it was asked for.

demonstrates pull-not-pushno-over-pulling
3 hints
  1. A generator function is already lazy: nothing between yields runs until the next value is pulled.
  2. take must stop pulling the moment it has enough, or an endless source never ends.
  3. for...of pulls a value before the body can decide it has enough. Calling .next() yourself puts you in control of when.
Solution
// naturals :: () -> Iterator Number
function* naturals() {
  let n = 0
  while (true) {
    yield n
    n += 1
  }
}

// take :: (Number, Iterator a) -> [a]
const take = (n, iterator) => {
  const it = iterator[Symbol.iterator]()
  const out = []
  while (out.length < n) {
    const step = it.next()
    if (step.done) break
    out.push(step.value)
  }
  return out
}
Starter · 2 exports: naturals, take
// naturals :: () -> Iterator Number
function* naturals() {
  // 0, 1, 2, ... forever
}

// take :: (Number, Iterator a) -> [a]
const take = (n, iterator) => {
  // pull exactly n values and stop
}

function* naturals() {
  let n = 0
  while (true) {
    yield n
    n += 1
  }
}
const take = (n, iterator) => {
  const out = []
  for (const value of iterator) {
    out.push(value)
    if (out.length >= n) break
  }
  return out
}

  }
}
const take = (n, iterator) => [...iterator].slice(0, n)

Filter an endless list

codeapply

Write firstSquaresOver, the first count perfect squares strictly greater than floor. The source stays endless, so only pull what you need.

demonstrates filter-lazilyno-over-pulling
2 hints
  1. Walk the naturals, square each one, and keep the ones past the floor.
  2. Stop as soon as you have collected enough.
Solution
function* naturals() {
  let n = 0
  while (true) {
    yield n
    n += 1
  }
}

// firstSquaresOver :: (Number, Number) -> [Number]
const firstSquaresOver = (floor, count) => {
  const out = []
  for (const n of naturals()) {
    const sq = n * n
    if (sq > floor) out.push(sq)
    if (out.length >= count) break
  }
  return out
}

const firstSquaresOver = (floor, count) => {
  const all = []
  for (let n = 0; n < 100; n++) all.push(n * n)
  return all.filter((sq) => sq > floor).slice(0, count)

IO

Composition & Flow 3 rubric 2 rungs 5 variants
  1. 2 Knows an IO is a description of an effect, and that building or mapping one performs nothing.
  2. 1 Can give IO a map and a chain, and knows chain must run the inner IO rather than wrapping it again.
  3. 1 Knows running the same IO twice performs the effect twice, and can say why that is the point.

Rungs

An IO that does nothing until you run it

codeimplement

IO wraps an effect as a value. Building and mapping over one must not perform the effect; only .run() does.

demonstrates description-not-actionmap-and-chainreusable
2 hints
  1. The whole IO is a function waiting to be called. map returns a new IO that wraps the old one.
  2. chain is like map, except the function already returns an IO, so run that one rather than wrapping it again.
Solution
// IO :: (() -> a) -> IO a
const IO = (effect) => ({
  run: effect,
  map: (f) => IO(() => f(effect())),
  chain: (f) => IO(() => f(effect()).run()),
  inspect: () => 'IO(?)'
})

Algebraic Effects

Composition & Flow 3 rubric 3 rungs 6 variants
  1. 2 Knows the program states what it needs rather than how to get it, and carries no knowledge of the handler.
  2. 1 Can write the interpreter that answers each request and feeds the answer back so the program can continue.
  3. 2 Can run one program under two handlers and get different behaviour without touching the program.

Rungs

Write the handler

codeimplement

A program written with algebraic effects asks for what it needs and lets someone else decide how to provide it. Write run, which drives a generator by answering each request it yields.

demonstrates drive-itswap-handlers
3 hints
  1. The generator yields a request like { type: "ask", key: "url" } and waits for an answer.
  2. Look the request type up in handlers, call it, and feed the answer back in with it.next(answer).
  3. Stop when the generator reports it is done, and hand back its return value.
Solution
// run :: (Generator, { [String]: (payload -> a) }) -> b
const run = (gen, handlers) => {
  const it = gen()
  let step = it.next()
  while (!step.done) {
    const request = step.value
    const handler = handlers[request.type]
    if (!handler) throw new Error(`No handler for ${request.type}`)
    step = it.next(handler(request))
  }
  return step.value
}
Starter · 1 export: run
// run :: (Generator, { [String]: (payload -> a) }) -> b
const run = (gen, handlers) => {
  // drive the generator, answering each request from handlers
}
3 variants that must fail

    handlers[step.value.type](step.value)
    step = it.next()
  }
  return step.value
}
const run = (gen, handlers) => {
  const it = gen()
  const step = it.next()
  if (step.done) return step.value
  return it.next(handlers[step.value.type](step.value)).value
}
const run = (gen, handlers) => {
  const it = gen()
  let step = it.next()
  let last
  while (!step.done) {
    last = handlers[step.value.type](step.value)
    step = it.next(last)
  }
  return last
}

What does the program know?

choicerecognize

A program written with algebraic effects yields { type: "ask_config", key: "url" }. What does it know about where the answer comes from?

demonstrates ask-do-not-do
  1. correct
    // Nothing. It states what it needs and something else decides how to supply it

    Separating the request from the interpretation is the whole idea. The same program runs against a real config or a fake one.

  2. wrong
    // That the answer comes from a file on disk

    If the program knew that, swapping in a test handler would mean rewriting it.

  3. wrong
    // That the handler is asynchronous

    Also a handler concern. The program just asks and waits to be resumed.

  4. wrong
    // That there is exactly one handler for it

    Any number of handlers can interpret the same request differently.

One program, two handlers

codeapply

greet is written for you and you may not change it. Write two handler sets so the same program talks to a real environment under one and to a fixture under the other. If the program has to change, it knew too much.

demonstrates ask-do-not-doswap-handlers
3 hints
  1. The program yields a request and waits. Whatever you send back becomes the value of the yield.
  2. runWith drives the generator: yield a request, look up the handler by its type, send the answer back, repeat until done.
  3. The two handler sets differ only in what they answer. Neither one edits greet.
Solution
// Do not change this. It states what it needs and knows nothing about how.
function* greet(id) {
  const name = yield { type: 'lookup', id }
  yield { type: 'log', message: 'greeting ' + name }
  return 'hello ' + name
}

// runWith :: (Handlers, Generator) -> a
const runWith = (handlers, gen) => {
  let step = gen.next()
  while (!step.done) {
    const request = step.value
    step = gen.next(handlers[request.type](request))
  }
  return step.value
}

// live :: Handlers
const live = {
  lookup: (request) => 'user-' + request.id,
  log: (request) => console.log(request.message)
}

// fixture :: Handlers
const fixture = {
  lookup: () => 'ada',
  log: () => undefined
}
Starter · 4 exports: greet, runWith, live, fixture

Side effects

Purity & Reasoning 3 rubric 3 rungs 5 variants
  1. 2 Can spot an effect whether it writes or reads, and knows reading the clock is as much an effect as writing to disk.
  2. 2 Can split a function that computes and acts into one that computes and one that acts.
  3. 2 Knows effects are not eliminated but relocated, and can say where they should end up.

Rungs

Spot every effect

choicerecognize

A side effect is any interaction with the world outside the function, read or write. Select every line below that has one.

demonstrates reads-count-too
  1. correct
    const now = Date.now()

    Reading the clock. The same call gives a different answer each time.

  2. wrong
    const doubled = xs.map(x => x * 2)

    map builds a new array and touches nothing outside.

  3. correct
    localStorage.setItem("k", v)

    Writing to storage, which outlives the call.

  4. correct
    cart.items.push(item)

    Mutating an object the caller still holds. They will see the change.

  5. wrong
    const total = items.reduce((a, i) => a + i.price, 0)

    Reads its argument and returns a number. Nothing escapes.

  6. correct
    console.log(total)

    Writing to the console is observable from outside, even though it feels harmless.

Reading is an effect too

codeapply

demonstrates reads-count-tooseparatepush-to-edge

Value

Purity & Reasoning 3 rubric 3 rungs 7 variants
  1. 2 Knows what can be a value in JavaScript, and that functions and classes are among them.
  2. 2 Can tell an expression from a statement, and knows only the former produces a value.
  3. 2 Can store operations in a data structure and look one up at runtime, rather than branching on a name.

Rungs

What can be a value?

choicerecognize

A value is anything that can be assigned to a variable, passed as an argument, and returned. Select everything that qualifies in JavaScript.

demonstrates what-qualifiesexpression-not-statement
  1. correct
    42

    The obvious case.

  2. correct
    (x) => x * 2

    Functions are values here. That is what makes higher-order functions possible.

  3. correct
    { name: "ada" }

    Objects are values, and can be passed around freely.

  4. correct
    [1, 2, 3]

    So are arrays.

  5. wrong
    if (x) { ... }

    A statement, not an expression. You cannot assign it to anything.

  6. correct
    class Point { }

    A class is a function underneath, so it too can be stored and passed.

Treat a function as data

codeimplement

Build registry, a plain object mapping names to operations, and apply, which looks one up and runs it. Functions really are just values you can store.

demonstrates functions-as-datawhat-qualifies
2 hints

  2. apply should say something useful when the name is not there rather than throwing.
Solution
// registry :: { [String]: (Number, Number) -> Number }
const registry = {
  add: (a, b) => a + b,
  sub: (a, b) => a - b,
  mul: (a, b) => a * b
}

// apply :: (String, Number, Number) -> Number | null
const apply = (name, a, b) => {
  const op = registry[name]
  return typeof op === 'function' ? op(a, b) : null
}

Constant

Purity & Reasoning 3 rubric 3 rungs 6 variants
  1. 2 Knows const stops the binding being reassigned and says nothing about the value it points at.
  2. 3 Knows Object.freeze protects one level only, and can say what is still writable underneath.
  3. 1 Can freeze a whole structure, including arrays and cycles, and return it for use inline.

Rungs

What does const actually promise?

choicerecognize

const stops the binding being reassigned. It says nothing about the value. Select every statement that is true.

demonstrates binding-not-valuefreeze-is-shallow
  1. correct
    const xs = [1, 2]
xs.push(3)   // allowed

    The binding still points at the same array. Its contents were never protected.

  2. correct
    const xs = [1, 2]
xs = [3]     // throws

    Reassigning the binding is exactly what const prevents.

  3. wrong
    Object.freeze({ a: { b: 1 } })
// makes the whole tree immutable

    freeze is shallow. The nested object is still writable.

  4. correct
    A frozen object silently ignores writes outside strict mode

    In sloppy mode the assignment just does nothing, which is why strict mode is worth having.

Freeze all the way down

codeimplement

Object.freeze only protects the top level. Write deepFreeze, which walks the whole structure.

demonstrates deep-freezefreeze-is-shallow
2 hints
  1. Freeze the object, then recurse into each of its values.
  2. Only objects and arrays can be frozen. A number or a string needs nothing done to it.
Solution
// deepFreeze :: a -> a
const deepFreeze = (o) => {
  if (o && typeof o === 'object' && !Object.isFrozen(o)) {
    Object.freeze(o)
    for (const key of Object.keys(o)) deepFreeze(o[key])
  }
  return o
}
Starter · 1 export: deepFreeze
// deepFreeze :: a -> a
const deepFreeze = (o) => {
  // freeze o and everything inside it
}

    for (const key of Object.keys(o)) Object.freeze(o[key])
  }
  return o
}
const deepFreeze = (o) => {
  if (o && typeof o === 'object' && !Object.isFrozen(o)) {
    Object.freeze(o)
    for (const key of Object.keys(o)) deepFreeze(o[key])
  }
}

Prove that const promises nothing about the value

codebreak

const stops the binding being reassigned and says nothing at all about what it points at. Demonstrate that: write mutateThrough, which changes a const-bound object without reassigning anything, and reassign, which reports what happens when you do try.

demonstrates binding-not-valuefreeze-is-shallow
3 hints
  1. const config = {...} then config.retries = 9 is legal. The binding did not move; the object did.
  2. reassign should try the assignment inside a try/catch and report the error's name, because that is the thing const actually prevents.
  3. shallowGap is about Object.freeze stopping at one level. Write to something one level down.
Solution
// mutateThrough :: () -> Object   change a const-bound object in place
const mutateThrough = () => {
  const config = { retries: 3 }
  config.retries = 9
  return config
}

// reassign :: () -> String   the name of the error you get for reassigning
const reassign = () => {
  const config = { retries: 3 }
  try {
    // eslint-disable-next-line no-const-assign
    eval('config = { retries: 9 }')
    return 'none'
  } catch (e) {
    return e.name
  }
}

// shallowGap :: () -> Boolean   did the nested write get through a freeze?
const shallowGap = () => {
  const config = Object.freeze({ limits: { retries: 3 } })
  config.limits.retries = 9
  return config.limits.retries === 9
}

Referential Transparency

Purity & Reasoning 3 rubric 3 rungs 6 variants
  1. 2 Can apply the test: could this call be replaced by its result without changing the program?
  2. 3 Can name the two things that break it, reading something that varies and changing something observable.
  3. 1 Can make a call substitutable by taking what it read as an argument and leaving its arguments alone.

Rungs

Which calls can be replaced by their result?

choicerecognize

An expression is referentially transparent when swapping it for its value changes nothing about the program. Select every call that qualifies.

demonstrates substitution-testwhat-breaks-it
  1. correct
    Math.max(2, 7)   // -> 7

    Always 7, and nothing happens on the way. Substituting it is invisible.

  2. wrong
    arr.pop()   // -> 3

    Replacing it with 3 would skip the shortening of arr, which the rest of the program can see.

  3. correct
    JSON.stringify({ a: 1 })   // -> '{"a":1}'

    Same input, same string, no trace left behind.

  4. wrong
    prompt("Name?")   // -> "ada"

    It asks a person. Replacing it with "ada" removes the asking.

  5. correct
    [1, 2].concat([3])   // -> [1, 2, 3]

    concat builds a new array and leaves both inputs alone.

  1. Anything addTax needs from outside should arrive as an argument.
  2. shift removes the element. Reading index 0 does not.
Solution

Write the substitution test

codeapply

The test is one question: could this call be replaced by its result without changing the program? Write substitutable, which answers it for a given function and arguments, and catches both of the things that break it.

demonstrates substitution-testwhat-breaks-it
3 hints
  1. Call it more than once with the same arguments. If the answers differ, the call cannot stand in for its result.
  2. T.effects is not available to you here. Use the watch helper in the starter: it records reads of the clock and the dice while your callback runs.
  3. The other breach is writing to what it was given. Compare the arguments before and after.
Solution
// watch :: (() -> a) -> { value, touchedOutside }
// Records whether the clock or the dice were read while fn ran.
const watch = (fn) => {
  const realNow = Date.now
  const realRandom = Math.random
  let touchedOutside = false
  Date.now = () => { touchedOutside = true; return realNow() }
  Math.random = () => { touchedOutside = true; return realRandom() }
  try {
    return { value: fn(), touchedOutside }
  } finally {
    Date.now = realNow
    Math.random = realRandom
  }
}

const snapshot = (v) => JSON.stringify(v)

// substitutable :: ((...a) -> b, [a]) -> Boolean
const substitutable = (fn, args) => {
  const before = snapshot(args)
  const first = watch(() => fn(...args))
  const second = watch(() => fn(...args))
  if (first.touchedOutside || second.touchedOutside) return false
  if (snapshot(args) !== before) return false
  return snapshot(first.value) === snapshot(second.value)
}

Equational Reasoning

Purity & Reasoning 3 rubric 3 rungs 6 variants
  1. 3 Can tell a rewrite that preserves meaning from one that quietly changes it, and can say which law licenses it.
  2. 2 Knows the rewrites only hold for pure functions, and can name a case where mutation breaks one.
  3. 1 Can fuse and reorder a pipeline using the laws, and check the result agrees on every input.

Rungs

Which rewrites are safe?

choicerecognize

Equational reasoning means replacing an expression with an equal one. Select every rewrite that is safe for pure f and g.

demonstrates safe-rewritesneeds-purity
  1. correct
    xs.map(f).map(g)
  ->  xs.map(x => g(f(x)))

    The functor composition law. One pass instead of two, same answer.

  2. wrong
    xs.filter(p).map(f)
  ->  xs.map(f).filter(p)

    The predicate now sees the mapped values, which are a different shape.

  3. correct
    xs.map(f).filter(p)
  ->  xs.filter(x => p(f(x))).map(f)

    Same elements survive and each gets the same f. Filtering first can even be cheaper.

  4. wrong
    xs.reverse().reverse()
  ->  xs

    reverse mutates in place, so the rewrite changes what the caller is holding. It would be safe for a copying reverse.

  5. correct
    xs.concat([]).length
  ->  xs.length

    concat with the empty list is the monoid identity, and it copies rather than mutating.

Rewrite a pipeline step by step

codeguided

Apply the laws to slow: fuse the two maps into one, and move the filter before the map so it runs on fewer elements. fast must give the same answers.

demonstrates apply-themsafe-rewrites
2 hints
  1. map(f).map(g) becomes map(x => g(f(x))).
  2. map(f).filter(p) becomes filter(x => p(f(x))).map(f). The predicate has to be rewritten to see the pre-map value.
Solution
const double = (n) => n * 2
const addTen = (n) => n + 10
const isBig = (n) => n > 20

const step = (n) => addTen(double(n))

// fast :: [Number] -> [Number]
const fast = (ns) => ns.filter((n) => isBig(step(n))).map(step)
Starter · 1 export: fast

const isBig = (n) => n > 20

const slow = (ns) => ns.map(double).map(addTen).filter(isBig)

// fast :: [Number] -> [Number]
// One map and one filter, and the filter runs first.
const fast = (ns) => ns.map(double).map(addTen).filter(isBig)

Find the input where a safe rewrite stops being safe

Replacing f(x) + f(x) with 2 * f(x) is the most ordinary optimisation there is, and it is only valid for pure functions. Write both forms and agree, which reports whether they match for a given function. You pass when it says yes for a pure one and no for an impure one.

demonstrates needs-puritysafe-rewrites

Idempotence

Purity & Reasoning 3 rubric 3 rungs 7 variants
  1. 3 Can state the law, f(f(x)) equals f(x), and classify a function against it.
  2. 2 Knows idempotent means applying it again changes nothing, not that it can be called safely twice.
  3. 1 Can make a normalizer idempotent, doing all of the work in the first pass rather than some of it each time.

Rungs

Which of these are idempotent?

choicerecognize

A function is idempotent when applying it again changes nothing: f(f(x)) equals f(x).

demonstrates the-lawnot-repetition
  1. correct
    const abs = (n) => Math.abs(n)

    The absolute value of an absolute value is the same number.

  2. wrong
    const inc = (n) => n + 1

    Every application moves further. f(f(0)) is 2, not 1.

  3. correct
    const sort = (xs) => [...xs].sort()

    Sorting a sorted list gives the same list back.

  4. correct
    const trim = (s) => s.trim()

    Once the whitespace is gone there is none left to remove.

  5. wrong
    const push = (xs) => [...xs, 0]

    Each application makes the list longer.

Make normalizeEmail idempotent

codeimplement

Normalizing an already-normalized address has to leave it alone. Trim the whitespace, lower-case it, and drop any +tag from the local part.

demonstrates make-it-sothe-law
2 hints
  1. Split on @, work on the local part, and put it back together.
  2. A +tag is everything from the first plus to the at-sign. Removing it twice must be the same as removing it once.
Solution
// normalizeEmail :: String -> String
const normalizeEmail = (email) => {
  const [local, domain] = email.trim().toLowerCase().split('@')
  if (domain === undefined) return local
  return `${local.split('+')[0]}@${domain}`
}

const normalizeEmail = (email) => {
  const [local, domain] = email.trim().toLowerCase().split('@')
  if (domain === undefined) return local
  const parts = local.split('+')
  return `${parts.slice(0, parts.length - 1).join('+') || parts[0]}@${domain}`
}

Tell idempotent apart from safe to repeat

demonstrates not-repetitionthe-law

Memoization

Purity & Reasoning 3 rubric 2 rungs 5 variants
  1. 1 Can cache by argument so each distinct input is computed once and earlier answers survive new ones.
  2. 1 Knows to ask whether the key is present rather than whether the value is truthy, so a cached 0 or undefined is kept.
  3. 1 Knows memoizing an impure function makes it report a stale answer, and can demonstrate it.

Rungs

Cache by argument

codeimplement

memoize should remember what a function returned for each argument, and never compute the same one twice.

demonstrates cache-per-argumentfalsy-results
2 hints
  1. A Map keyed by the argument is enough for a single-argument function.
  2. Check whether the key is present rather than whether the stored value is truthy, or a cached 0 recomputes forever.
Solution
// memoize :: (a -> b) -> (a -> b)
const memoize = (fn) => {
  const cache = new Map()
  return (x) => {
    if (!cache.has(x)) cache.set(x, fn(x))
    return cache.get(x)
  }
}
Starter · 1 export: memoize
// memoize :: (a -> b) -> (a -> b)
const memoize = (fn) => {
  // remember the answer for each argument
}
3 variants that must fail
const memoize = (fn) => {
  const cache = new Map()
  return (x) => {
    if (!cache.get(x)) cache.set(x, fn(x))
    return cache.get(x)
  }
}
const memoize = (fn) => {
  let lastArg
  let lastResult
  return (x) => {
    if (x !== lastArg) {
      lastArg = x
      lastResult = fn(x)
    }
    return lastResult
  }
}
const memoize = (fn) => {
  let result
  return (x) => {
    if (result === undefined) result = fn(x)
    return result
  }
}

Watch memoization lie

demonstrates needs-purity
2 hints
  1. A pure function of its argument can be memoized safely. Make yours depend on something else.
  2. It still has to take an argument, or there is nothing to key on.
Solution
let counter = 0
// Depends on counter, not just on prefix, so a cache keyed on prefix is wrong.
const nextId = (prefix) => {
  counter += 1
  return `${prefix}-${counter}`
}

// which is exactly what a cache cannot preserve.

Contracts

Purity & Reasoning 3 rubric 2 rungs 3 variants
  1. 1 Can guard both what goes in and what comes out, and knows checking only the inputs leaves half the promise unkept.
  2. 2 Knows the value is in where it fails, so the inputs must be checked before the function runs.
  3. 1 Can throw a message that names which side and which position broke, so the caller does not have to guess.

Rungs

Wrap a function in a contract

codeimplement

A contract states what a function accepts and what it promises. Write withContract, which checks the inputs before the call and the output after.

demonstrates both-sidesfail-at-the-boundaryuseful-message
2 hints
  1. Run every input predicate first, then call, then check the result.
  2. Throw a TypeError naming which side of the contract broke, so the message is worth reading.
Solution
// withContract :: ([a -> Boolean], b -> Boolean, f) -> f
const withContract = (inputChecks, outputCheck, fn) => (...args) => {
  args.forEach((arg, i) => {
    const check = inputChecks[i]
    if (check && !check(arg)) {
      throw new TypeError(`Argument ${i} did not meet the contract: ${String(arg)}`)
    }
  })
  const result = fn(...args)
  if (!outputCheck(result)) {
    throw new TypeError(`Result did not meet the contract: ${String(result)}`)
  }
  return result
}
Starter · 1 export: withContract
// withContract :: ([a -> Boolean], b -> Boolean, f) -> f
const withContract = (inputChecks, outputCheck, fn) => {
  // check the arguments, call fn, check the result
}
3 variants that must fail
const withContract = (inputChecks, outputCheck, fn) => (...args) => {
  args.forEach((arg, i) => {
    const check = inputChecks[i]
    if (check && !check(arg)) throw new TypeError(`Argument ${i} did not meet the contract`)
  })
  return fn(...args)
}
const withContract = (inputChecks, outputCheck, fn) => (...args) => {
  const result = fn(...args)
  if (!outputCheck(result)) throw new TypeError('Result did not meet the contract')
  return result
}
const withContract = (inputChecks, outputCheck, fn) => (...args) => {
  const result = fn(...args)
  args.forEach((arg, i) => {
    const check = inputChecks[i]
    if (check && !check(arg)) throw new TypeError(`Argument ${i} did not meet the contract`)
  })
  if (!outputCheck(result)) throw new TypeError('Result did not meet the contract')
  return result
}

What is a contract for?

choicerecognize

Pick the best description of what a runtime contract buys you.

demonstrates fail-at-the-boundary
  1. correct
    // It fails at the boundary that broke the promise,
// instead of somewhere further downstream

    The value of a contract is where it fails, not that it fails. A bad argument is caught at the door.

  2. wrong
    // It makes the function faster

    It adds work on every call. You trade a little speed for a better failure.

  3. wrong
    // It replaces a type system

    It overlaps, but a contract runs at runtime on real values and a type checker runs before that.

  4. wrong
    // It makes the function pure

    Unrelated. Throwing on a bad input does not remove any effect the function already had.

Type Signatures

Types & Data Modeling 3 rubric 3 rungs 7 variants
  1. 2 Can read a Hindley-Milner signature, and knows each arrow is one argument arriving.
  2. 2 Can tell whether an implementation matches its signature, including the order the arguments arrive in.
  3. 2 Knows a signature over any a forbids looking inside, and can say what that leaves a function able to do.

Rungs

Which implementation fits the signature?

choicerecognize

The signature is map :: (a -> b) -> [a] -> [b]. Pick the implementation that matches it.

demonstrates read-the-arrowsmatch-implementation
  1. wrong
    const map = (fn, xs) => xs.map(fn)

    The arrows say one argument at a time. This takes both at once.

  2. correct
    const map = (fn) => (xs) => xs.map(fn)

    A function in, then a list, then a list out. Each arrow is one step.

  3. wrong
    const map = (xs) => (fn) => xs.map(fn)

    The arguments arrive in the wrong order: the signature asks for the function first.

  4. wrong
    const map = (fn) => (xs) => xs.forEach(fn)

    forEach returns undefined, and the signature promises a [b].

Write the functions the signatures describe

codeimplement

Each signature below determines almost everything about its function. Write the implementation each one describes.

demonstrates signature-constrainsread-the-arrows
3 hints
  1. a -> a can only hand its argument back. There is nothing else it could return.
  2. a -> b -> a has to return the first argument: it is the only a it has.
  3. [a] -> Number cannot look inside the elements, because it knows nothing about a.
Solution
// identity :: a -> a
const identity = (a) => a

// first :: a -> b -> a
const first = (a) => (b) => a

// count :: [a] -> Number
const count = (xs) => xs.length

// flip :: (a -> b -> c) -> b -> a -> c
const flip = (f) => (b) => (a) => f(a)(b)

Write the only function each signature allows

A signature made of type variables leaves almost nothing to decide. Write the three functions so each one works for every type at once, which is what a variable in a signature means.

demonstrates match-implementationsignature-constrains

Algebraic data type

Types & Data Modeling 3 rubric 3 rungs 7 variants
  1. 2 Can classify a type as a sum or a product, and say which word in its description gave it away.
  2. 2 Can count the inhabitants of a composite type, multiplying for products and adding for sums.
  3. 1 Knows the empty product has one inhabitant and the empty sum has none, and can say why.

Rungs

Sum or product?

choicerecognize

A product holds several things at once. A sum is one of several possibilities. Select every type below that is a sum.

demonstrates sum-vs-product
  1. wrong
    // { width :: Number, height :: Number }

    A width AND a height. That is a product.

  2. correct
    // Circle Number | Square Number

    A shape is one or the other, never both.

  3. wrong
    // [Number, String]

    A tuple holds both positions at once.

  4. correct
    // Loading | Ok a | Failed e

    Three possibilities, one at a time.

  5. correct
    // Boolean

    true or false. The smallest sum worth naming.

Count the inhabitants

codeimplement

The names are arithmetic. A product multiplies the possibilities of its parts; a sum adds them. Write the two counters.

demonstrates countingidentities
3 hints
  1. A pair of a Boolean and a three-valued type has 2 times 3 possible values.
  2. A choice between a Boolean and a three-valued type has 2 plus 3.
  3. An empty product has one inhabitant: the empty tuple. An empty sum has none.
Solution
// productSize :: [Number] -> Number
const productSize = (sizes) => sizes.reduce((a, b) => a * b, 1)

// sumSize :: [Number] -> Number
const sumSize = (sizes) => sizes.reduce((a, b) => a + b, 0)

Count the values, and watch the names make sense

codeapply

The names are arithmetic. Write countProduct and countSum over the sizes of the parts, then sizeOf, which handles a small description of either kind. The answers are why one is called a product and the other a sum.

demonstrates sum-vs-productcounting
3 hints
  1. A product holds all of its fields at once, so the combinations multiply.
  2. A sum holds exactly one of its cases, so the possibilities add.
  3. A product of no fields has one value, not none: there is exactly one way to hold nothing. That makes 1 the right starting point for a multiplication, and 0 for a sum.
Solution
// countProduct :: [Number] -> Number
const countProduct = (sizes) => sizes.reduce((a, b) => a * b, 1)

// countSum :: [Number] -> Number
const countSum = (sizes) => sizes.reduce((a, b) => a + b, 0)

// sizeOf :: { kind, parts } -> Number
// { kind: 'product', parts: [2, 3] }  ->  6
// { kind: 'sum', parts: [2, 3] }      ->  5
const sizeOf = (type) =>
  type.kind === 'product' ? countProduct(type.parts) : countSum(type.parts)

Product type

Types & Data Modeling 3 rubric 2 rungs 3 variants
  1. 1 Knows a product holds every field at once, and can count its inhabitants by multiplying. recognition only
  2. 1 Can produce every combination of a product's fields, and knows the count is the product of the field sizes.
  3. 1 Knows a field with no possible values makes the whole product impossible, and the empty product has exactly one value.

Rungs

How many values does this type have?

choicerecognize

Given type Flags = { admin :: Boolean, active :: Boolean, role :: "read" | "write" | "own" }, how many distinct values can it take?

demonstrates all-at-once
  1. correct
    12

    2 times 2 times 3. A product multiplies the possibilities of its fields.

  2. wrong
    7

    That would be adding them. Addition is what a sum type does.

  3. wrong
    3

    That is just the role. The two Booleans vary independently of it.

  4. wrong
    2

    Only one field accounted for.

Build every inhabitant

codeimplement

Write inhabitants, which takes the possible values of each field and produces every combination. Its length should be the product of the field sizes.

demonstrates enumeratezero-field
2 hints
  1. Start with one empty combination and extend it one field at a time.
  2. For each combination so far, and each value of the next field, make a longer combination.
Solution
// inhabitants :: [[a]] -> [[a]]
const inhabitants = (fields) =>
  fields.reduce(
    (acc, values) => acc.flatMap((combo) => values.map((v) => [...combo, v])),
    [[]]
  )

  // every way of picking one value from each field

const inhabitants = (fields) =>
  fields.reduce(
    (acc, values) =>
      acc.flatMap((combo) =>
        values.map((v) => {
          combo.push(v)
          return combo
        })
      ),
    [[]]
  )

Sum type

Types & Data Modeling 4 rubric 3 rungs 6 variants typed lap
  1. 3 Can build a tagged union where a value is exactly one case and carries that case's payload.
  2. 2 Can write a match that refuses to run unless every case is handled, and knows why a silent undefined is the failure to prevent.
  3. 1 Can identify a sum type in the wild, including recursive ones like a list. recognition only
  4. 1 Can say how a discriminated union turns a forgotten case into a compile error, and what the never parameter has to do with it.

Lap 1 · JavaScript

A tagged union with an exhaustive match

codeimplement

A sum type is a value that is one of several shapes. Build the three constructors and a match that refuses to run unless every case is handled.

demonstrates one-of-severalexhaustive-match
2 hints
  1. Each constructor tags its value: { type: "loading" }, { type: "ok", data }, { type: "failed", error }.
  2. match looks the handler up by tag. Missing handlers should throw, not silently return undefined.
Solution
const Loading = () => ({ type: 'loading' })
const Ok = (data) => ({ type: 'ok', data })
const Failed = (error) => ({ type: 'failed', error })

const CASES = ['loading', 'ok', 'failed']

// match :: (State, { loading, ok, failed }) -> a
const match = (state, handlers) => {
  const missing = CASES.filter((c) => typeof handlers[c] !== 'function')
  if (missing.length) {
    throw new TypeError(`match is missing a handler for: ${missing.join(', ')}`)
  }
  return handlers[state.type](state)
}

}

}

Which of these are sum types?

choicerecognize

A sum type is "one of these". Select every example.

demonstrates recognizeone-of-several
  1. correct
    // Either e a  is Left e or Right a

    Exactly two shapes, and a value is one of them.

  2. wrong
    // { x :: Number, y :: Number }

    A point has an x AND a y. That is a product.

  3. correct
    // Option a  is Some a or None

    One of two shapes again.

  4. correct
    // Boolean  is true or false

    The smallest interesting sum type: two shapes, neither carrying anything.

  5. correct
    // [a]  a list of values

    Empty or a head followed by a tail. A list is a sum type that happens to be recursive.

Lap 2 · With types

Match on Status exhaustively

codeimplementlap 2 · ts

The union is given. Write assertNever and render so every case is handled and an unexpected tag is loud rather than silent.

demonstrates one-of-severalexhaustive-matchtyped-signature
3 hints
  1. assertNever takes a never and throws. Its whole job is to be uncallable once every case is handled.
  2. Handle all three tags, then call assertNever in the default branch with the value itself.
  3. Reaching the default branch at runtime means something got past the type, so it should throw, not return.
Solution
type Status =
  | { tag: 'loading' }
  | { tag: 'ok', data: string }
  | { tag: 'failed', reason: string }

const assertNever = (x: never): never => {
  throw new Error('unhandled case: ' + JSON.stringify(x))
}

const render = (s: Status): string => {
  switch (s.tag) {
    case 'loading': return 'spinner'
    case 'ok': return s.data
    case 'failed': return s.reason
    default: return assertNever(s)
  }
}
Starter · 2 exports: render, assertNever

  | { tag: 'failed', reason: string }

  return undefined as never
}

  switch (s.tag) {
    case 'loading': return 'spinner'
    default: return ''
  }
}
