Option

Types & Data Modeling 4 rubric 3 rungs 8 variants typed lap
  1. 2 Knows Option makes "there might be nothing" part of the type, and that map on None skips the function entirely.
  2. 2 Can write map, chain and getOrElse, and knows chain is for a function that already returns an Option.
  3. 2 Can chain a sequence of lookups so a miss anywhere short-circuits, with getOrElse as the only way out.
  4. 1 Can read an Option as a tagged union and say why getOrElse returns a plain value while map does not.

Lap 1 · JavaScript

Some, None, and the three ways out

codeimplement

Option makes "there might be nothing here" part of the type. Give it map, chain, and getOrElse, so a missing value flows through without a single null check.

demonstrates absence-in-the-typemap-chain-getorelse
3 hints
  1. map on None does nothing and stays None. On Some it applies the function and rewraps.
  2. chain is for a function that itself returns an Option, so do not wrap the result again.
  3. getOrElse is the only way out. It is where the default finally appears.
Solution
// Some :: a -> Option a
const Some = (value) => ({
  isSome: true,
  value,
  map: (f) => Some(f(value)),
  chain: (f) => f(value),
  getOrElse: (fallback) => value,
  inspect: () => `Some(${JSON.stringify(value)})`
})

// None :: () -> Option a
const None = () => ({
  isSome: false,
  map: (f) => None(),
  chain: (f) => None(),
  getOrElse: (fallback) => fallback,
  inspect: () => 'None'
})

codeapply

Using the Option given, write cityOf, which reads user.address.city and falls back to "unknown" when any step is missing.

demonstrates one-exitmap-chain-getorelse

  map: () => None(),
  chain: () => None(),
  getOrElse: (fallback) => fallback

  o != null && o[k] != null ? Some(o[k]) : None()

  prop('address')(user).chain(prop('city')).getOrElse('unknown')

Lap 2 · With types

Satisfy Option<A>

codeimplementlap 2 · ts

The type is given. Write fromNullable, map and getOrElse so that absence survives a map and only getOrElse hands back a bare value.

demonstrates absence-in-the-typeone-exittyped-signature
3 hints
  1. Test o.tag === 'some' before touching o.value. In the other branch there is no value to touch.
  2. map returns Option<B>. On the empty case there is nothing to apply f to, so hand the empty case straight back.
  3. Only null and undefined count as absent. 0 and '' are values.
Solution
type Option<A> =
  | { tag: 'some', value: A }
  | { tag: 'none' }

const fromNullable = <A>(a: A | null | undefined): Option<A> =>
  a === null || a === undefined ? { tag: 'none' } : { tag: 'some', value: a }

const map = <A, B>(f: (a: A) => B, o: Option<A>): Option<B> =>
  o.tag === 'some' ? { tag: 'some', value: f(o.value) } : o

const getOrElse = <A>(fallback: A, o: Option<A>): A =>
  o.tag === 'some' ? o.value : fallback

  a === null || a === undefined ? { tag: 'none' } : { tag: 'some', value: a }

const map = <A, B>(f: (a: A) => B, o: Option<A>): Option<B> =>
  o.tag === 'some' ? { tag: 'some', value: f(o.value) } : o

const getOrElse = <A>(fallback: A, o: Option<A>): A =>
  (o.tag === 'some' ? o : fallback) as A

Either

Types & Data Modeling 4 rubric 3 rungs 8 variants typed lap
  1. 2 Knows Either carries a failure that says why, unlike Option which only says there was one.
  2. 3 Knows map and chain work on the Right only, so a Left passes through untouched, and fold runs exactly one branch.
  3. 1 Can build a pipeline where each step fails with its own message, and the first failure ends it.
  4. 1 Can read Either<E, A> and point at where right-bias lives: map names only A in its signature, so the failure side cannot be touched.

Lap 1 · JavaScript

Left, Right, and fold

codeimplement

Either carries a success or a failure, and says which. map and chain work on the Right only; fold is how you finally handle both.

demonstrates carries-the-reasonright-biased
2 hints
  1. Left is the failure. Mapping over it changes nothing and keeps the error intact.
  2. fold(onLeft, onRight) runs exactly one of the two, depending which side you are on.
Solution
// Left :: e -> Either e a
const Left = (error) => ({
  isRight: false,
  map: (f) => Left(error),
  chain: (f) => Left(error),
  fold: (onLeft, onRight) => onLeft(error),
  inspect: () => `Left(${JSON.stringify(error)})`
})

// Right :: a -> Either e a
const Right = (value) => ({
  isRight: true,
  map: (f) => Right(f(value)),
  chain: (f) => f(value),
  fold: (onLeft, onRight) => onRight(value),
  inspect: () => `Right(${JSON.stringify(value)})`
})

A parse pipeline that reports why it failed

codeapply

Write parseAge: the input must be valid JSON, must have an age, and that age must be a positive number. Each failure gets its own message.

demonstrates distinct-failuresright-biased
2 hints
  1. Wrap JSON.parse in a try/catch and turn the throw into a Left.
  2. Chain the three steps. The first Left ends it, and its message survives to the end.
Solution
const Left = (error) => ({
  isRight: false,
  map: () => Left(error),
  chain: () => Left(error),
  fold: (onLeft) => onLeft(error)
})
const Right = (value) => ({
  isRight: true,
  map: (f) => Right(f(value)),
  chain: (f) => f(value),
  fold: (onLeft, onRight) => onRight(value)
})

const parseJson = (s) => {
  try {
    return Right(JSON.parse(s))
  } catch (e) {
    return Left('not json')
  }
}

const getAge = (o) => (o != null && 'age' in o ? Right(o.age) : Left('no age'))

const checkPositive = (n) =>
  typeof n === 'number' && n > 0 ? Right(n) : Left('age must be positive')

// parseAge :: String -> Either String Number
const parseAge = (json) => parseJson(json).chain(getAge).chain(checkPositive)

Lap 2 · With types

Satisfy Either<E, A>

codeimplementlap 2 · ts

The type is given. Write map, mapLeft and fold so each one touches only the side its signature names.

demonstrates carries-the-reasonright-biasedtyped-signature
3 hints
  1. map returns Either<E, B>. E is unchanged, so a left passes through exactly as it arrived.
  2. mapLeft is the mirror image. Same shape, other tag.
  3. fold returns a bare B. Both handlers produce one, which is why there is nothing left to unwrap.
Solution
type Either<E, A> =
  | { tag: 'left', left: E }
  | { tag: 'right', right: A }

const map = <E, A, B>(f: (a: A) => B, e: Either<E, A>): Either<E, B> =>
  e.tag === 'right' ? { tag: 'right', right: f(e.right) } : e

const mapLeft = <E, A, F>(f: (e: E) => F, e: Either<E, A>): Either<F, A> =>
  e.tag === 'left' ? { tag: 'left', left: f(e.left) } : e

const fold = <E, A, B>(onLeft: (e: E) => B, onRight: (a: A) => B, e: Either<E, A>): B =>
  e.tag === 'left' ? onLeft(e.left) : onRight(e.right)

Lens

Types & Data Modeling 4 rubric 3 rungs 7 variants typed lap
  1. 3 Knows a lens is a getter and setter travelling together, and that the setter must build a new structure.
  2. 1 Can write view, set and over, and knows over is set composed with view.
  3. 1 Can compose two lenses to reach a nested field, updating it without disturbing anything around it.
  4. 1 Can read a lens's type and see that the setter has to return a whole new structure, not a fragment of one.

Lap 1 · JavaScript

lens, view, set, over

codeimplement

A lens is a getter and a setter travelling together. Build one, and the three functions that use it.

demonstrates getter-and-setterview-set-over
3 hints
  1. lens(getter, setter) just holds the pair.
  2. over is set composed with view: read it, apply the function, write it back.
  3. The setter must build a new object rather than writing into the old one.
Solution
// lens :: ((s -> a), (a -> s -> s)) -> Lens s a
const lens = (getter, setter) => ({ getter, setter })

// view :: (Lens s a, s) -> a
const view = (l, s) => l.getter(s)

// set :: (Lens s a, a, s) -> s
const set = (l, value, s) => l.setter(value, s)

// over :: (Lens s a, (a -> a), s) -> s
const over = (l, f, s) => set(l, f(view(l, s)), s)

// lensProp :: String -> Lens s a
const lensProp = (key) =>
  lens(
    (s) => s[key],
    (value, s) => ({ ...s, [key]: value })
  )

Update something nested

codeapply

Compose two lenses into one that reaches user.address.city, then use it to update a deeply nested record without touching anything else.

demonstrates composegetter-and-setter

Solution
const lens = (getter, setter) => ({ getter, setter })
const view = (l, s) => l.getter(s)
const set = (l, value, s) => l.setter(value, s)

  lens(
    (s) => view(inner, view(outer, s)),
    (value, s) => set(outer, set(inner, value, view(outer, s)), s)
  )

// cityLens :: Lens User String
const cityLens = composeLens(lensProp('address'), lensProp('city'))

// renameCity :: (String, User) -> User
const renameCity = (city, user) => set(cityLens, city, user)

Lap 2 · With types

A lens with its types written down

codeimplementlap 2 · ts

Build lensProp against this interface. The setter's return type is the part to read carefully.

demonstrates typed-signaturegetter-and-setter
2 hints
  1. Lens<S, A> means: the whole is S, the part is A. The setter gives back an S.

Solution
interface Lens<S, A> {
  getter: (s: S) => A
  setter: (value: A, s: S) => S

  getter: (s) => s[key],
  setter: (value, s) => ({ ...s, [key]: value })

Prism

Types & Data Modeling 4 rubric 3 rungs 6 variants typed lap
  1. 2 Knows a prism focuses on a case that might not be there, unlike a lens which always finds its focus.
  2. 2 Can write a matching pair where preview and review undo each other, and knows only exactly round-tripping values may match.
  3. 1 Can reject the inputs that nearly parse, such as trailing characters, padding, decimals, and the empty string.
  4. 1 Can read a prism's type and see that the match may fail, because preview hands back an Option and review does not.

Lap 1 · JavaScript

A prism for integers in strings

codeimplement

Where a lens always finds its focus, a prism might not. Build one that reads an integer out of a string when there is one, and rebuilds the string from an integer.

demonstrates round-tripguard-the-edges
3 hints
  1. preview returns Some(n) when the string is a whole number, and None() otherwise.
  2. review goes the other way and always succeeds.
  3. Only a string that round-trips exactly should match: "007" reviews back to "7", so it must not match.
Solution
const Some = (value) => ({ isSome: true, value })
const None = () => ({ isSome: false })

// preview :: String -> Option Number
const preview = (s) => {
  const n = Number(s)
  // Only match when rebuilding gives back exactly what we were handed,
  // so "007" and " 7 " stay out.
  return Number.isInteger(n) && String(n) === s ? Some(n) : None()
}

// review :: Number -> String
const review = (n) => String(n)

Lens or prism?

choicerecognize

What separates a prism from a lens?

demonstrates may-not-match
  1. correct
    // A lens always finds its focus; a prism might not

    A lens is for a field that is always there. A prism is for a case that may not match.

  2. wrong
    // A prism is read-only

    Both read and write. review is a prism writing.

  3. wrong
    // A prism only works on strings

    It works on any sum type. Picking the Right out of an Either is a prism.

  4. wrong
    // A lens composes and a prism does not

    Both compose, with each other too. That is most of why optics are interesting.

Lap 2 · With types

Satisfy Prism<string, number>

codeimplementlap 2 · ts

The interface is given. Fill in numeric so that preview finds a whole number inside a string when there is one, and review puts it back. Only a well-formed number counts: '007' is not one.

demonstrates may-not-matchround-triptyped-signature
3 hints
  1. preview returns an Option, never a bare number. That is the whole reason a prism is not a lens.
  2. /^(0|[1-9]\d*)$/ accepts '0' and '42' and rejects '007' and 'abc'.
  3. review never fails, so it needs no Option on the way out.
Solution
type Option<A> = { tag: 'some', value: A } | { tag: 'none' }

interface Prism<S, A> {
  preview: (s: S) => Option<A>
  review: (a: A) => S
}

const numeric: Prism<string, number> = {
  preview: (s) =>
    /^(0|[1-9]\d*)$/.test(s) ? { tag: 'some', value: Number(s) } : { tag: 'none' },
  review: (n) => String(n)
}

  preview: (s: S) => Option<A>
  review: (a: A) => S
}

const numeric: Prism<string, number> = {
  preview: (s) => ({ tag: 'none' }),
  review: (n) => ''
}

const numeric: Prism<string, number> = {
  preview: (s) =>
    /^\d+$/.test(s) ? { tag: 'some', value: Number(s) } : { tag: 'none' },
  review: (n) => String(n)
}
type Option<A> = { tag: 'some', value: A } | { tag: 'none' }

interface Prism<S, A> {
  preview: (s: S) => Option<A>
  review: (a: A) => S
}

const numeric: Prism<string, number> = {
  preview: (s) =>
    /^(0|[1-9]\d*)$/.test(s) ? { tag: 'some', value: Number(s) } : { tag: 'none' },
  review: (n) => n
}

Iso

Types & Data Modeling 4 rubric 3 rungs 5 variants typed lap
  1. 2 Knows an isomorphism needs both round trips to hold, and can name a pair that satisfies only one.
  2. 2 Can tell a lossless conversion from one that discards information, and knows rounding is never an isomorphism.
  3. 1 Knows a float round trip needs a tolerance, and that testing with whole numbers can let a lossy pair pass.
  4. 1 Can read an iso's type and see that neither direction can fail, because there is no Option on either arrow.

Lap 1 · JavaScript

Two views of the same thing

codeimplement

An isomorphism is a pair of conversions that lose nothing. Write the Celsius/Fahrenheit pair, and the pair between a record and a tuple.

demonstrates losslessfloat-tolerance
2 hints
  1. Fahrenheit is c * 9 / 5 + 32, and back again is (f - 32) * 5 / 9.
  2. The record and the tuple hold exactly the same information, so neither direction can drop a field.

// toF :: Number -> Number

// toPair :: { x, y } -> [Number, Number]
const toPair = (coords) => [coords.x, coords.y]

// toCoords :: [Number, Number] -> { x, y }
const toCoords = (pair) => ({ x: pair[0], y: pair[1] })

Which pairs are isomorphisms?

choicerecognize

An isomorphism loses nothing in either direction. Select every pair that qualifies.

demonstrates both-round-trips
  1. correct
    // [a, b]  <->  { first: a, second: b }

    Same information in both shapes, and either direction recovers the other exactly.

  2. wrong
    // Number  <->  String   via String() and Number()

    Not quite. "007" and "7" both become 7, so going back cannot know which you started with.

  3. correct
    // Boolean  <->  0 | 1

    Two values on each side, matched one to one.

  4. wrong
    // Number  <->  Number   via Math.round

    Rounding throws away the fraction, and nothing can put it back.

  5. correct
    // Option a  <->  a | null

    Some maps to the value, None to null. Nothing is lost, provided the value itself is never null.

Traversal

Types & Data Modeling 4 rubric 3 rungs 6 variants typed lap
  1. 2 Knows a traversal focuses on any number of elements, where a lens focuses on one and a prism on at most one.
  2. 2 Can modify the matching elements while leaving the non-matching ones exactly where they were.
  3. 1 Knows reading after modifying should agree with modifying what was read, and that identity must change nothing.
  4. 1 Can read a traversal's type and see that reading gives many values while writing still gives back one whole structure.

Lap 1 · JavaScript

Focus on many things at once

codeimplement

Where a lens focuses on one thing and a prism on at most one, a traversal focuses on any number. Write one over the even numbers in a list.

demonstrates leave-the-restconsistency
2 hints
  1. getAll collects the elements that match, in order.
  2. modify applies the function to the matching elements and leaves the others exactly as they were.
Solution
const isEven = (n) => n % 2 === 0

// getAll :: [Number] -> [Number]
const getAll = (xs) => xs.filter(isEven)

// modify :: ((Number -> Number), [Number]) -> [Number]
const modify = (f, xs) => xs.map((x) => (isEven(x) ? f(x) : x))
Starter · 2 exports: getAll, modify

}

// modify :: ((Number -> Number), [Number]) -> [Number]
const modify = (f, xs) => {
}
3 variants that must fail

const modify = (f, xs) => xs.filter(isEven).map(f)
const isEven = (n) => n % 2 === 0
const getAll = (xs) => xs.filter(isEven)
const modify = (f, xs) => xs.map(f)
const isEven = (n) => n % 2 === 0
const getAll = (xs) => xs.filter(isEven)
const modify = (f, xs) => {
  xs.forEach((x, i) => {
    if (isEven(x)) xs[i] = f(x)
  })
  return xs
}

How many does each optic focus on?

choicerecognize

Match the optic to the number of things it can focus on.

demonstrates many-focuses
  1. correct
    // lens: exactly one
// prism: zero or one
// traversal: zero or more

    That is the whole hierarchy. Each is a loosening of the one before.

  2. wrong
    // lens: one
// prism: many
// traversal: one

    Prism and traversal are the wrong way round. A prism is about a case that may not match.

  3. wrong
    // All three focus on exactly one

    Then there would be no reason to have three.

  4. wrong
    // lens: zero or more
// prism: one
// traversal: zero or one

    All three are shuffled.

Lap 2 · With types

Satisfy Traversal<Player, number>

codeimplementlap 2 · ts

The interface is given. Fill in scores so it reads every score off a player and can map over all of them at once, leaving the name where it was.

demonstrates many-focusesleave-the-resttyped-signature

  3. Nothing here is allowed to write into the player you were handed.
Solution
interface Player { name: string; scores: number[] }

  getAll: (s: S) => A[]
  modify: (f: (a: A) => A, s: S) => S
}

const scores: Traversal<Player, number> = {
  getAll: (p) => p.scores,
  modify: (f, p) => ({ ...p, scores: p.scores.map(f) })
}

Lambda Calculus

Types & Data Modeling 3 rubric 2 rungs 3 variants
  1. 1 Knows the whole system is variables, abstraction and application, and that numbers and conditionals are encoded rather than built in. recognition only
  2. 1 Can read a Church numeral as "apply f this many times" and build succ and add from that reading.
  3. 1 Knows where the encoding meets real values, and that only the conversion back needs a real number.

Rungs

Numbers made only of functions

codeguided

A Church numeral is a number encoded as "apply this function n times". Build zero, succ, add, and toInt with nothing but functions.

demonstrates church-numeralsescape-hatch
3 hints
  1. zero applies f no times: (f) => (x) => x. one applies it once.
  2. succ(n) applies f once more than n does: (f) => (x) => f(n(f)(x)).
  3. toInt is the escape hatch: run the numeral with "add one" and a starting point of 0.
Solution
// zero :: Church  applies f zero times
const zero = (f) => (x) => x

// succ :: Church -> Church  applies f once more
const succ = (n) => (f) => (x) => f(n(f)(x))

// add :: Church -> Church -> Church
const add = (m) => (n) => (f) => (x) => m(f)(n(f)(x))

// toInt :: Church -> Number
const toInt = (n) => n((k) => k + 1)(0)
Starter · 4 exports: zero, succ, add, toInt

}

const succ = (n) => (f) => (x) => {
}

// add :: Church -> Church -> Church
const add = (m) => (n) => (f) => (x) => {
}

// toInt :: Church -> Number
const toInt = (n) => {
}

const succ = (n) => (f) => (x) => f(n(f)(x))

const toInt = (n) => n((k) => k + 1)(0)

What is the lambda calculus made of?

choicerecognize

Pick the complete list of what the untyped lambda calculus has.

demonstrates three-rules
  1. correct
    // Variables, abstraction (\x. body), and application (f x)

    Three rules, and everything else, numbers included, is built out of them.

  2. wrong
    // Variables, numbers, and arithmetic

    Numbers are not primitive. Church numerals encode them out of functions.

  3. wrong
    // Functions, conditionals, and recursion

    Conditionals and recursion are both encodable, not built in. The Y combinator gives you recursion.

  4. wrong
    // Variables, abstraction, application, and types

    The untyped version has no types at all. Types come with the typed variants.

Setoid

Algebraic Structures 4 rubric 3 rungs 6 variants typed lap
  1. 2 Can give a type an equals that compares contents rather than identity.
  2. 2 Can state reflexivity, symmetry and transitivity, and say which one a given definition breaks.
  3. 2 Knows equals answers true or false, and that an ordering dressed as an equality fails symmetry.
  4. 1 Can read equals and see that both arguments name the same type variable, so comparing two different types is a compile error rather than a silent false.

Lap 1 · JavaScript

Equality you can rely on

codeimplement

A Setoid has an equals that is reflexive, symmetric, and transitive. Give Point one that compares by contents rather than by identity.

demonstrates by-contentsthree-lawsboolean
2 hints
  1. === on two objects asks whether they are the same object, which is not what you want here.
  2. Compare the coordinates.
Solution
// Point :: (Number, Number) -> Setoid
const Point = (x, y) => ({
  x,
  y,
  equals: (other) => other.x === x && other.y === y,
  inspect: () => `Point(${x}, ${y})`
})

  x, y,
  equals: (other) => other === Point(x, y),
  inspect: () => `Point(${x}, ${y})`
})

  x, y,
  equals: (other) => other.x === x,
  inspect: () => `Point(${x}, ${y})`
})
const Point = (x, y) => ({
  x, y,
  equals: (other) => other.x >= x && other.y >= y,
  inspect: () => `Point(${x}, ${y})`
})

Which law does this break?

choicerecognize

A team defines equals: (other) => other.score >= this.score. Which Setoid law fails first?

demonstrates three-laws
  1. correct
    // Symmetry: a.equals(b) must match b.equals(a)

    With scores 1 and 2, the first says yes and the second says no. Reflexivity and transitivity both survive.

  2. wrong
    // Reflexivity: a.equals(a) must be true

    A score is always at least itself, so this one holds.

  3. wrong
    // Transitivity

    It holds too, since the comparison chains. Symmetry is the one that goes.

  4. wrong
    // None of them

    Symmetry fails as soon as the two scores differ.

Semigroup

Algebraic Structures 4 rubric 3 rungs 6 variants typed lap
  1. 2 Can write a concat that returns the same type, so three values can be combined in one chain.
  2. 2 Knows associativity is the only requirement, and can name operations that have it and ones that do not.
  3. 2 Knows one type can be a semigroup in several ways, and that the wrapper is what picks which.
  4. 1 Can read concat and say that the three As are what closure means: whatever goes in, the same type comes out.

Lap 1 · JavaScript

Three ways to combine two things

codeimplement

A Semigroup has a concat that is associative. Write three of them over numbers: keep the larger, keep the smaller, keep the first.

demonstrates concat-stays-insideassociativityseveral-instances
2 hints
  1. Each concat takes another value of the same type and gives one back.
  2. Associativity is what makes them semigroups. First is associative even though it ignores its argument.
Solution
const Max = (value) => ({
  value,
  concat: (other) => Max(value > other.value ? value : other.value),
  inspect: () => `Max(${value})`
})

const Min = (value) => ({
  value,
  concat: (other) => Min(value < other.value ? value : other.value),
  inspect: () => `Min(${value})`
})

const First = (value) => ({
  value,
  concat: (other) => First(value),
  inspect: () => `First(${value})`
})

Which operations are associative?

choicerecognize

A semigroup needs associativity and nothing else. Select every operation that has it.

demonstrates associativity
  1. correct
    (a, b) => a + b

    Addition groups either way.

  2. wrong
    (a, b) => a - b

    (1 - 2) - 3 is -4, but 1 - (2 - 3) is 2.

  3. correct
    (a, b) => Math.max(a, b)

    The largest of three is the largest however you group them.

  4. wrong
    (a, b) => a / b

    Division is not associative either. Try 8, 4, 2.

  5. correct
    (a, b) => a.concat(b)  // string concat

    Joining text groups either way.

  6. correct
    (a, b) => a

    Always keeping the left one is associative, if not very useful.

Lap 2 · With types

Satisfy Semigroup<number> three times

demonstrates concat-stays-insideseveral-instancestyped-signature

  2. firstSemigroup ignores its second argument. That is allowed, and it is still associative.
  3. Nothing here may return anything but a number, or the result could not be combined again.

interface Semigroup<A> {
  concat: (a: A, b: A) => A

Monoid

Algebraic Structures 4 rubric 3 rungs 7 variants typed lap
  1. 3 Can supply an empty that is neutral on both sides, and knows the wrong one annihilates instead.
  2. 2 Knows the identity is what gives a fold of the empty list an answer.
  3. 1 Can show that an operation may have a one-sided identity and no two-sided one.
  4. 1 Can read Monoid<A> extends Semigroup<A> and say that the added empty is a value of type A, not a function, and that is the whole difference.

Lap 1 · JavaScript

A semigroup with a neutral element

codeimplement

A Monoid is a Semigroup plus an empty that changes nothing on either side. Write Sum, Product, All, and Any.

demonstrates identityempty-fold
2 hints
  1. The empty element is whatever leaves the other side alone: 0 for addition, 1 for multiplication.
  2. For All the neutral value is true, because "and true" changes nothing. For Any it is false.
Solution
const Sum = (value) => ({ value, concat: (o) => Sum(value + o.value), inspect: () => `Sum(${value})` })
Sum.empty = () => Sum(0)

const Product = (value) => ({ value, concat: (o) => Product(value * o.value), inspect: () => `Product(${value})` })
Product.empty = () => Product(1)

const All = (value) => ({ value, concat: (o) => All(value && o.value), inspect: () => `All(${value})` })
All.empty = () => All(true)

const Any = (value) => ({ value, concat: (o) => Any(value || o.value), inspect: () => `Any(${value})` })
Any.empty = () => Any(false)

Functor

Algebraic Structures 5 rubric 4 rungs 8 variants typed lap
  1. 2 Knows map applies a function inside and hands back the same kind of container, so mapping can continue.
  2. 2 Can state the identity and composition laws and say what each one rules out.
  3. 1 Knows a functor may decline to apply the function, as Maybe does on nothing, and still obey both laws.
  4. 1 Can read the functor signature and build something that satisfies it, seeing that map changes the contents while the container stays the same.
  5. 1 Can construct something that looks like a functor and quietly fails a law, and know which one it failed.

Lap 1 · JavaScript

Give Box a lawful map

codeimplement

A functor is a container with a map that obeys two laws: mapping identity changes nothing, and mapping f then g equals mapping their composition.

demonstrates map-rewrapstwo-laws

  2. Box(f(value)) applies the function and wraps the result again.

  inspect: () => `Box(${JSON.stringify(value)})`
})

A Maybe that skips missing values

Maybe is a functor whose map does nothing when the value is null or undefined. Use it to read a nested property without crashing.

demonstrates skipping-is-lawfultwo-laws

Solution
const Maybe = (value) => ({
  value,
  isNothing: () => value === null || value === undefined,
  map: (f) =>
    value === null || value === undefined ? Maybe(value) : Maybe(f(value)),
  inspect: () => `Maybe(${JSON.stringify(value)})`
})

Break a law on purpose

codebreakinverted

Write BadBox whose map still returns a BadBox but quietly breaks a functor law. You pass when the law checker catches it.

demonstrates break-a-law
2 hints
  1. Keeping the shape right is easy. The interesting failures are the ones that still look like a functor.
  2. A map that depends on anything other than its argument and the value inside will not compose.
Solution
let calls = 0
const BadBox = (value) => ({
  value,
  // Depends on how many times map has ever run, so mapping twice is not
  // the same as mapping the composition.
  map: (f) => BadBox(f(value) + calls++),
  inspect: () => `BadBox(${JSON.stringify(value)})`
})
Starter · 1 export: BadBox
const BadBox = (value) => ({
  value,
  map: (f) => BadBox(f(value)),
  inspect: () => `BadBox(${JSON.stringify(value)})`
})
2 variants that must fail
const BadBox = (value) => ({
  value,
  map: (f) => BadBox(f(value)),
  inspect: () => `BadBox(${JSON.stringify(value)})`
})
const BadBox = (value) => ({
  value,
  map: (f) => f(value),
  inspect: () => `BadBox(${JSON.stringify(value)})`
})

What is of allowed to do?

choicerecognize

Pick the rule of has to follow.

demonstrates why-it-mattersneutral
  1. correct
    // Put the value in the container and nothing else

    of is the neutral way in. Anything extra would break the laws that build on it.

  2. wrong
    // Validate the value and reject bad ones

    That would make of partial, and the applicative laws assume it always succeeds.

  3. wrong
    // Flatten the value if it is already a container

    of always adds exactly one layer. Removing one is what chain does.

  4. wrong
    // Pick the most appropriate case for the value

    Tempting for something like Either, but of has to be predictable. It always goes to the same side.

Constant Functor

Algebraic Structures 4 rubric 3 rungs 5 variants typed lap

  2. 2 Knows both functor laws hold precisely because nothing happens, and can say why.
  3. 1 Can say what Const buys you: accumulating a value while a generic traversal runs, which is how a lens getter is built. recognition only

What is Const good for?

choicerecognize

A functor that ignores every function sounds useless. What does it actually buy you?

demonstrates what-it-is-for
  1. correct
    // It collects a value while a generic traversal walks a structure,
// which is how a lens getter is built

    Run a traversal with Const and the payload accumulates while the mapping does nothing. That is the trick behind van Laarhoven lenses.

  2. wrong
    // It makes mapping faster

    It does skip the work, but nobody reaches for Const for speed.

  3. wrong
    // It stops the value ever being read

    The value is perfectly readable. It is the mapping that is inert.

  4. wrong
    // It is a placeholder with no real use

    It earns its place in optics, where the same traversal serves as both a getter and a setter depending which functor you hand it.

Show that it is not a lawful monad

codebreakinverted

demonstrates left-identity-fails
3 hints
  1. Left identity says of(a).chain(f) equals f(a).
  2. Const drops f, so the left side always carries whatever of put there. Pick an f that carries something else.
  3. Return the pieces: a starting value, and a function whose Const carries a different payload.
Solution
const Const = (value) => ({
  value,
  map: () => Const(value),
  chain: () => Const(value),
  inspect: () => `Const(${JSON.stringify(value)})`
})

const a = 1

// of(a).chain(f) keeps "", but f(a) carries "something".
// No definition of of can fix this: chain discards f, so the left
// side can never see what f wanted to carry.
const f = (x) => Const('something')

  value,
  map: () => Const(value),
  chain: () => Const(value),
  inspect: () => `Const(${JSON.stringify(value)})`
})

// of has to produce a Const without a payload to carry,
// so it falls back on something neutral.
const of = (x) => Const('')

// A starting value:
const a = 1

// A function a -> Const, carrying something of its own:
const f = (x) => Const('')

Lift

Algebraic Structures 4 rubric 3 rungs 5 variants typed lap
  1. 1 Can write liftA2 once and have it work on any applicative, because it uses only map and ap.
  2. 2 Knows the function has to be curried before ap, since ap supplies one argument at a time.
  3. 3 Knows lifting must not read a field like .value, and can say why that would restrict it to one shape.
  4. 1 Can read liftA2's type and say why the function has to be curried before ap can be used, and why the typed version has to name its container where the JavaScript one did not.

  1. The same definition works for anything with map and ap. That is the point of writing it once.
  2. ma.map(curried).ap(mb).
Solution

const liftA2 = (f) => (ma) => (mb) => ma.map((a) => (b) => f(a, b)).ap(mb)

What does lifting need from the container?

choicerecognize

One liftA2 serves every applicative. What does it rely on?

demonstrates never-reach-inside
  1. correct
    // map and ap, and nothing else

    It never looks inside. That is exactly why the same definition works for Box, Array, Maybe and the rest.

  2. wrong
    // A .value field to read

    Then it would only work for containers shaped that way, which defeats the purpose.

  3. wrong
    // chain

    chain is stronger than needed. Applicative is enough, and some things have ap without chain.

  4. wrong
    // A way to compare two containers

    Useful for testing the laws, but lifting itself never compares anything.

Lap 2 · With types

Write liftA2 over Box

codeimplementlap 2 · ts

Box is given, with map and ap. Write liftA2 so an ordinary two-argument function can work on two wrapped values without ever unwrapping either.

demonstrates curry-firstnever-reach-insidetyped-signature
3 hints
  1. Map over the first box with something that takes an a and gives back a function still waiting for a b.
  2. After that map you are holding a Box<(b: B) => C>, which is exactly what ap wants as its receiver.
  3. If .value appears anywhere in your answer, you left the container.
Solution
interface Box<A> {
  value: A
  map: <B>(f: (a: A) => B) => Box<B>
  ap: <B, C>(this: Box<(b: B) => C>, other: Box<B>) => Box<C>
}

const box = <A>(value: A): Box<A> => ({
  value,
  map: (f) => box(f(value)),
  ap<B, C>(this: Box<(b: B) => C>, other: Box<B>) {
    return other.map(this.value)
  }
})

Applicative Functor

Algebraic Structures 4 rubric 3 rungs 8 variants typed lap
  1. 2 Can apply a function that is itself inside a container, and knows the receiver holds the function.
  2. 2 Can apply a curried function to several wrapped values in sequence.
  3. 2 Knows map can be written from of and ap, so every applicative is a functor, and can say what ap adds.
  4. 1 Can read ap's type and say why it only makes sense when the container holds a function, and can name what TypeScript cannot say about applicatives in general.

Monad

Algebraic Structures 5 rubric 3 rungs 8 variants typed lap
  1. 3 Knows chain is for a function that already returns a container, so the result is not wrapped twice.
  2. 1 Can build a chain where a failure partway through skips the rest.
  3. 2 Knows chain removes exactly one layer, and can show it on a container of containers.
  4. 1 Can state the two identity laws and associativity, and check an instance against them.
  5. 1 Can read map and chain side by side and say which one would have given a doubly wrapped value, from the signatures alone.

Solution
// chain :: ((a -> [b]), [a]) -> [b]
const chain = (f, xs) => xs.reduce((acc, x) => acc.concat(f(x)), [])

// pairs :: ([a], [b]) -> [[a, b]]
const pairs = (as, bs) => chain((a) => bs.map((b) => [a, b]), as)

Lap 2 · With types

Satisfy Maybe<A>

codeimplementlap 2 · ts

The interface is given. Write just and nothing so that chain returns one layer where map would have returned two.

demonstrates chain-does-not-rewrapflatten-one-leveltyped-signature
3 hints
  1. map's f gives back a bare value, so you have to wrap it. chain's f gives back a Maybe already.
  2. If your chain body has a call to just in it, look at the return type again.
  3. nothing owes a Maybe<B> from both, and never runs f for either.
Solution
interface Maybe<A> {
  map: <B>(f: (a: A) => B) => Maybe<B>
  chain: <B>(f: (a: A) => Maybe<B>) => Maybe<B>
  getOrElse: (fallback: A) => A
}

const just = <A>(value: A): Maybe<A> => ({
  map: (f) => just(f(value)),
  chain: (f) => f(value),
  getOrElse: () => value
})

const nothing = <A>(): Maybe<A> => ({
  map: () => nothing(),
  chain: () => nothing(),
  getOrElse: (fallback) => fallback
})

Kleisli Composition

Algebraic Structures 4 rubric 3 rungs 5 variants typed lap
  1. 2 Can say why ordinary composition cannot join two functions that each return a container.
  2. 2 Can write composeK, and knows it reads right to left the way compose does.
  3. 1 Knows a failure in the first step skips the second entirely, and that the composition is associative.
  4. 1 Can write ordinary compose and Kleisli compose side by side and point at the exact place the types stop lining up.

Lap 1 · JavaScript

Compose functions that return containers

codeimplement

Ordinary composition cannot join a -> M b with b -> M c, because the second takes a b and gets an M b. composeK bridges that with chain.

demonstrates compose-with-chainshort-circuits
2 hints
  1. Run the first function, then chain the second onto its result.
  2. Like compose, it reads right to left: the rightmost function runs first.
Solution
// composeK :: ((b -> M c), (a -> M b)) -> (a -> M c)
const composeK = (g, f) => (a) => f(a).chain(g)
Starter · 1 export: composeK
// composeK :: ((b -> M c), (a -> M b)) -> (a -> M c)
const composeK = (g, f) => {
}
2 variants that must fail
const composeK = (g, f) => (a) => g(a).chain(f)
const composeK = (g, f) => (a) => f(a).map(g)

Why not ordinary composition?

choicerecognize

Given half :: Number -> Maybe Number, why does compose(half, half) not work?

demonstrates why-compose-fails
  1. correct
    // The second half receives a Maybe Number, but it expects a Number

    The shapes do not meet. composeK uses chain to unwrap between the steps.

  2. wrong
    // half is not curried

    Currying is unrelated. The problem is the shape of what comes out.

  3. wrong
    // Maybe has no map

    It does, and map is what makes the doubly wrapped version. chain is what avoids it.

  4. wrong
    // compose only takes two functions

    Variadic compose is easy to write. The mismatch would still be there.

Lap 2 · With types

Write composeK

codeimplementlap 2 · ts

The types are given. Write composeK so two functions that each return a Maybe join into one that returns a single Maybe.

demonstrates why-compose-failscompose-with-chaintyped-signature
3 hints
  1. Run f first. What you get back is a Maybe<B>, not a B.
  2. g is typed (b: B) => Maybe<C>. Look at chain's signature and see which operation takes a function of that shape.
  3. If your result is a Maybe inside a Maybe, you reached for map where chain was the only thing that fits.
Solution
interface Maybe<A> {
  map: <B>(f: (a: A) => B) => Maybe<B>
  chain: <B>(f: (a: A) => Maybe<B>) => Maybe<B>
  getOrElse: (fallback: A) => A
}

const just = <A>(value: A): Maybe<A> => ({
  map: (f) => just(f(value)),
  chain: (f) => f(value),
  getOrElse: () => value
})

const nothing = <A>(): Maybe<A> => ({
  map: () => nothing(),
  chain: () => nothing(),
  getOrElse: (fallback) => fallback
})

type Kleisli<A, B> = (a: A) => Maybe<B>

const composeK = <A, B, C>(g: Kleisli<B, C>, f: Kleisli<A, B>): Kleisli<A, C> =>
  (a) => f(a).chain(g)

})

type Kleisli<A, B> = (a: A) => Maybe<B>

const composeK = <A, B, C>(g: Kleisli<B, C>, f: Kleisli<A, B>): Kleisli<A, C> =>
  (a) => (g as unknown as Kleisli<A, C>)(a)
interface Maybe<A> {
  map: <B>(f: (a: A) => B) => Maybe<B>
  chain: <B>(f: (a: A) => Maybe<B>) => Maybe<B>
  getOrElse: (fallback: A) => A
}

const just = <A>(value: A): Maybe<A> => ({
  map: (f) => just(f(value)),
  chain: (f) => f(value),
  getOrElse: () => value
})

const nothing = <A>(): Maybe<A> => ({
  map: () => nothing(),
  chain: () => nothing(),
  getOrElse: (fallback) => fallback
})

type Kleisli<A, B> = (a: A) => Maybe<B>

const composeK = <A, B, C>(g: Kleisli<B, C>, f: Kleisli<A, B>): Kleisli<A, C> =>
  (a) => (g as unknown as Kleisli<A, B>)(a).chain(f as never) as never

Comonad

Algebraic Structures 4 rubric 3 rungs 5 variants typed lap
  1. 2 Knows a comonad is a monad with the arrows reversed: extract takes a value out where of puts one in.
  2. 2 Can write extend, and knows the function it takes receives the whole container rather than the value inside.
  3. 1 Can check the two identity laws and associativity for a comonad instance.
  4. 1 Can put a monad's signatures next to a comonad's and show that every arrow has turned around.

Lap 1 · JavaScript

extract and extend

codeimplement

A Comonad is a Monad with the arrows turned round. Where of puts a value in, extract takes one out; where chain takes a function that wraps, extend takes one that unwraps.

demonstrates extend-sees-contextlaws
2 hints
  1. extract simply hands the value back.
  2. extend(f) calls f with the whole container, not with the value inside, and wraps the answer.
Solution
// CoIdentity :: a -> CoIdentity a
const CoIdentity = (value) => ({
  value,
  map: (f) => CoIdentity(f(value)),
  extract: () => value,
  extend: (f) => CoIdentity(f(CoIdentity(value))),
  inspect: () => `CoIdentity(${JSON.stringify(value)})`
})

Monad or comonad?

choicerecognize

Which pair of signatures belongs to a Comonad?

demonstrates arrows-reversed
  1. correct
    // extract :: w a -> a
// extend  :: (w a -> b) -> w a -> w b

    Both point out of the container. The function extend takes consumes a container and produces a plain value.

  2. wrong
    // of    :: a -> m a
// chain :: (a -> m b) -> m a -> m b

    That is the Monad pair, with the arrows the other way.

  3. wrong
    // extract :: a -> w a
// extend  :: (b -> w a) -> w a -> w b

    extract points the wrong way. It takes a value out, not in.

  4. wrong
    // map :: (a -> b) -> w a -> w b
// ap  :: w (a -> b) -> w a -> w b

    Functor and Apply. Every comonad is a functor, but these are not what makes it one.

Free Monad

Algebraic Structures 3 rubric 2 rungs 3 variants
  1. 1 Knows a free monad turns a program into data, so nothing happens until an interpreter runs it. recognition only
  2. 1 Can add an instruction that carries what an interpreter needs to act on it.
  3. 1 Can run one program under two interpreters and get different behaviour with no change to the program.

Rungs

Add an instruction to the program

codeapply

A Free Monad turns a program into data, so the same program can be interpreted in more than one way. Add a Read instruction and teach both interpreters about it.

demonstrates instruction-carriesmany-interpreters
2 hints
  1. An instruction is a plain object with a tag and a payload.
  2. The interpreter is a lookup from tag to behavior. Each one answers Read differently.
Solution
const Write = (text) => ({ type: 'write', text })

// Read :: String -> Instruction
const Read = (key) => ({ type: 'read', key })

const interpret = (program, handlers) =>
  program.map((instruction) => handlers[instruction.type](instruction))

// runReal :: ([Instruction], Object) -> [String]
const runReal = (program, store) =>
  interpret(program, {
    write: (i) => i.text,
    read: (i) => String(store[i.key])
  })

// runTest :: [Instruction] -> [String]
const runTest = (program) =>
  interpret(program, {
    write: (i) => `WROTE: ${i.text}`,
    read: () => 'stub'
  })

What does "free" buy you?

choicerecognize

Why build a program as data rather than just calling the functions?

demonstrates program-as-data
  1. correct
    // The same program can be interpreted more than one way:
// really, in a test, or logged without running at all

    Separating the description from the interpretation is the whole trade. A test interpreter needs no mocking.

  2. wrong
    // It runs faster

    It runs slower. You are paying for flexibility.

  3. wrong
    // It makes the instructions pure

    Building the description is pure, but the real interpreter still performs the effects. They have just moved.

  4. wrong
    // It removes the need for a monad

    The opposite. It gives you a monad for free from any functor, which is where the name comes from.

Monad Transformer

Algebraic Structures 3 rubric 2 rungs 2 variants
  1. 1 Knows two monads do not compose in general, so each one ships a transformer that knows how to sit on another. recognition only
  2. 1 Can chain through a stacked monad in one step rather than unwrapping each layer.
  3. 1 Knows chain must unwrap what the function returned, or the layers multiply.

Rungs

Stack Maybe on top of another monad

codeapply

Two monads do not compose on their own. A transformer knows how to sit on top of one: MaybeT gives you Maybe behavior inside whatever it wraps, with one chain instead of two.

demonstrates one-chainunwrap-the-result
2 hints
  1. A MaybeT holds an inner monad whose value is a Maybe.
  2. chain unwraps the inner monad, then looks at the Maybe. On Nothing it stops; on Just it runs f and takes its inner value.
Solution
const Just = (value) => ({ isNothing: false, value })
const Nothing = () => ({ isNothing: true })

const Id = (value) => ({
  value,
  map: (f) => Id(f(value)),
  chain: (f) => f(value)
})

// MaybeT :: m (Maybe a) -> MaybeT m a
const MaybeT = (inner) => ({
  inner,
  map: (f) =>
    MaybeT(inner.map((maybe) => (maybe.isNothing ? Nothing() : Just(f(maybe.value))))),
  chain: (f) =>
    MaybeT(
      inner.chain((maybe) =>
        maybe.isNothing ? Id(Nothing()) : f(maybe.value).runMaybeT()
      )
    ),
  runMaybeT: () => inner
})

const Nothing = () => ({ isNothing: true })

  value,
  map: (f) => Id(f(value)),
  chain: (f) => f(value)
})

  map: (f) => MaybeT(inner.map((m) => Just(f(m.value)))),
  chain: (f) => MaybeT(inner.chain((m) => (m.isNothing ? Id(Nothing()) : f(m.value).runMaybeT()))),
  runMaybeT: () => inner
})

Why do transformers exist?

choicerecognize

What problem does a monad transformer solve?

demonstrates monads-do-not-compose
  1. correct
    // Monads do not compose in general, so each one ships a
// transformer that knows how to sit on top of another

    Two functors always compose. Two monads do not, so the knowledge has to be supplied per monad.

  2. wrong
    // They make monads faster

    They add a layer of wrapping. Speed is not the trade.

  3. wrong
    // They turn any functor into a monad

    That is the Free monad.

  4. wrong
    // They let a monad be used without chain

    Chain is exactly what they give you: one instead of two.

Bifunctor

Algebraic Structures 4 rubric 3 rungs 8 variants typed lap
  1. 2 Can map both positions of a two-slot structure, each with its own function.
  2. 3 Knows bimap maps the contents and never changes which slot or which case a value is in.
  3. 1 Can use bimap on Either to adjust the failure without touching the success path.
  4. 1 Can read bimap's type and see that the two functions cannot be crossed, because each one names a different type variable.

Contravariant Functor

Algebraic Structures 4 rubric 3 rungs 5 variants typed lap
  1. 2 Knows contramap applies its function on the way in, adapting what the structure accepts rather than what it produces.
  2. 2 Knows the composition law reverses, and can say why chaining contramaps runs the last one first.
  3. 1 Knows two of these are compared by what they do on sample inputs, because their whole content is a function.
  4. 1 Can read contramap's type and see the arrow pointing the other way, (b: B) => A where map would have (a: A) => B.

Lap 1 · JavaScript

Map over the input instead of the output

codeimplement

A Predicate consumes values rather than producing them, so a function is applied before it runs, not after. That is contramap.

demonstrates maps-the-inputbehavioural-equality
2 hints
  1. map would apply f to the result. contramap applies it to the argument on the way in.
  2. Predicate((x) => run(f(x))).
Solution
// Predicate :: (a -> Boolean) -> Predicate a
const Predicate = (run) => ({
  run,
  // contramap :: (b -> a) -> Predicate b
  contramap: (f) => Predicate((x) => run(f(x))),
  inspect: () => 'Predicate(?)'
})
Starter · 1 export: Predicate
// Predicate :: (a -> Boolean) -> Predicate a

  run,
  // contramap :: (b -> a) -> Predicate b
  contramap: (f) => {
  },
  inspect: () => 'Predicate(?)'
})
2 variants that must fail

  run,
  contramap: (f) => Predicate((x) => f(run(x))),
  inspect: () => 'Predicate(?)'
})
const Predicate = (run) => ({
  run,
  contramap: (f) => Predicate(run),
  inspect: () => 'Predicate(?)'
})

Which way does composition go?

choicerecognize

For a functor, u.map(f).map(g) equals u.map(x => g(f(x))). What is the equivalent for contramap?

demonstrates order-reverses
  1. correct
    u.contramap(f).contramap(g)  ===  u.contramap(x => f(g(x)))

    The order reverses. Each contramap adds a step earlier in the pipeline, so the last one added runs first.

  2. wrong
    u.contramap(f).contramap(g)  ===  u.contramap(x => g(f(x)))

    That is the covariant order. Reading it that way is the classic mistake.

  3. wrong
    u.contramap(f).contramap(g)  ===  u.contramap(f)

    Then the second contramap would do nothing.

  4. wrong
    There is no composition law for contramap

    There is, and it is the reversed one. Both laws mirror the functor laws exactly.

Lap 2 · With types

codeimplementlap 2 · ts

  3. If you find yourself wanting to call f on the result of run, check the arrow direction again. run gives a boolean, and f does not take one.

  run,
  contramap: (f) => predicate((b) => run(f(b)))
})

  contramap: (f) => predicate(run) as never

Profunctor

Algebraic Structures 4 rubric 3 rungs 5 variants typed lap
  1. 2 Can adapt the input and the output of a function-like structure in one step, with the input function running first.
  2. 2 Knows it is contravariant in what it consumes and covariant in what it produces.
  3. 2 Knows the input functions compose in reverse and the output functions compose forwards.
  4. 1 Can read promap's type and see one arrow pointing in and one pointing out, and say which end each function adapts.

Lap 1 · JavaScript

Adjust both ends of a function

codeimplement

A Profunctor consumes on one side and produces on the other. promap(f, g) pre-processes the input with f and post-processes the output with g.

demonstrates both-endscomposition-order
2 hints
  1. f runs first, on the way in. g runs last, on the way out.
  2. (x) => g(run(f(x))).
Solution

})

Which end is which?

choicerecognize

A Profunctor P a b consumes an a and produces a b. How does it vary in each?

demonstrates variance
  1. correct
    // Contravariant in a, covariant in b

    The input side takes a function pointing the other way, and the output side takes one pointing the usual way.

  2. wrong
    // Covariant in both

    That would be a bifunctor, not a profunctor.

  3. wrong
    // Contravariant in both

    Then you could not post-process the result.

  4. wrong
    // Covariant in a, contravariant in b

    The right idea, the wrong way round. The consuming side is the contravariant one.

Lap 2 · With types

Satisfy Fn<A, B>

codeimplementlap 2 · ts

The interface is given. Fill in fn so promap adapts the input with f on the way in and the output with g on the way out.

demonstrates both-endsvariancecomposition-ordertyped-signature
3 hints
  1. The result is Fn<C, D>, so the function you build takes a C. Start there and work inward.
  2. f turns that C into an A, which is what run accepts. g turns what run gives back into a D.
  3. Three calls nested: g(run(f(c))). Get them in any other order and the types do not line up.
Solution
interface Fn<A, B> {
  run: (a: A) => B
  promap: <C, D>(f: (c: C) => A, g: (b: B) => D) => Fn<C, D>
}

const fn = <A, B>(run: (a: A) => B): Fn<A, B> => ({
  run,
  promap: (f, g) => fn((c) => g(run(f(c))))
})

Alternative

Algebraic Structures 4 rubric 3 rungs 7 variants typed lap
  1. 3 Can write an alt that keeps the first thing that has something, and a zero that steps aside.
  2. 1 Knows zero must be neutral on both sides, and that mapping over it does nothing.
  3. 2 Can try several sources in order and take the first that has the value, with present-but-falsy still counting as present.
  4. 1 Can read alt and say why it returns a wrapped value while getOrElse returns a bare one, and when each is the one you want.

Try several sources in order

const lookup = (key) => (source) =>
  key in source ? Just(source[key]) : Nothing()

  sources.map(lookup(key)).reduce((acc, m) => acc.alt(m), Nothing())

demonstrates first-successchain-fallbackstyped-signature
3 hints
  1. A just already has a value. Its alt has no reason to look at the argument.
  2. A nothing has nothing of its own, so its alt hands back whatever it was given, as it is.
  3. alt returns a Maybe<A>, so whatever you return has to be one, ready for the next alt.

interface Maybe<A> {

  getOrElse: (fallback: A) => A

  getOrElse: () => value

  alt: (other) => other,
  getOrElse: (fallback) => fallback

Foldable

Algebraic Structures 4 rubric 3 rungs 6 variants typed lap
  1. 2 Can give a structure a reduce that visits every element exactly once in a defined order.
  2. 1 Knows the traversal order is part of the contract, and that reduce must agree with the structure's own idea of its elements.
  3. 2 Can say what one reduce provides: sum, length, contains, maximum, and anything else expressible as a fold.
  4. 1 Can read reduce's type and say that the seed is what fixes B, which is why one fold can produce a number, a list, or a string.

Lap 1 · JavaScript

Fold a tree down to one value

codeimplement

Anything Foldable can be collapsed to a single value. Give a binary tree a reduce that visits its elements left to right.

demonstrates collapseorder-matters
2 hints
  1. A leaf contributes its own value. A node contributes its left subtree, then itself, then its right.
  2. Thread the accumulator through in that order.
Solution
// Leaf :: a -> Tree a
const Leaf = (value) => ({
  reduce: (f, seed) => f(seed, value),
  toArray: () => [value],
  inspect: () => `Leaf(${JSON.stringify(value)})`
})

// Node :: (Tree a, a, Tree a) -> Tree a
const Node = (left, value, right) => ({
  reduce: (f, seed) => right.reduce(f, f(left.reduce(f, seed), value)),
  toArray: () => [...left.toArray(), value, ...right.toArray()],
  inspect: () => 'Node(...)'
})

What does Foldable let you write once?

choicerecognize

Once a structure has reduce, what comes for free?

demonstrates what-it-buys
  1. correct
    // sum, length, toArray, contains, maximum, and anything else
// expressible as a fold

    One method buys a whole family, and none of them need to know the shape of the structure.

  2. wrong
    // map

    reduce cannot rebuild the structure it walked. Mapping needs Functor.

  3. wrong
    // A way to compare two structures

    Only via their elements, and that is what Setoid is for.

  4. wrong
    // Lazy traversal

    A fold visits everything. Stopping early needs something else.

Satisfy Tree<A>

codeimplementlap 2 · ts

The interface is given. Write leaf and node so the fold visits left before right and threads the accumulator through both.

demonstrates collapsewhat-it-buystyped-signature
3 hints
  1. A leaf has one value. Apply f to the seed and that value, and that is the answer.
  2. node folds the left side first. What comes back is a B, which is exactly what the right side needs as its seed.
  3. You never build an array on the way. The seed is the only accumulator there is.

interface Tree<A> {

})

  reduce: (f, seed) => right.reduce(f, left.reduce(f, seed))
})

Traversable

Algebraic Structures 5 rubric 3 rungs 8 variants typed lap
  1. 2 Can turn a structure of containers into a container of the structure, so the check happens once.
  2. 3 Knows one failure sinks the whole thing, and that filtering the failures out is a different operation.
  3. 2 Knows an empty structure succeeds, because nothing failed.
  4. 1 Can map and sequence in one pass, and use it to validate a list.
  5. 1 Can read sequence's type as a layer swap, and name what has to be true for the swap to be possible at all.

Turn the structure inside out

  return Just(out)

traverse in one pass

codeapply

traverse(f, xs) maps and sequences together. Use it to validate a list of inputs, failing on the first bad one.

demonstrates traverseall-or-nothing
1 hint
  1. Apply f to each element, then sequence the results. One pass is enough.
Solution
const Just = (value) => ({ isNothing: false, value, map: (f) => Just(f(value)) })
const Nothing = () => ({ isNothing: true, map: () => Nothing() })

// traverse :: ((a -> Maybe b), [a]) -> Maybe [b]
const traverse = (f, xs) => {
  const out = []
  for (const x of xs) {
    const m = f(x)
    if (m.isNothing) return Nothing()
    out.push(m.value)
  }
  return Just(out)
}

const parseNum = (s) => {
  const n = Number(s)
  return Number.isInteger(n) && s.trim() !== '' ? Just(n) : Nothing()
}

// parseAll :: [String] -> Maybe [Number]
const parseAll = (strings) => traverse(parseNum, strings)

Lap 2 · With types

Write sequence over an array of Maybes

codeimplementlap 2 · ts

The types are given. Write sequence so an array of Maybes becomes one Maybe of an array, with a single answer for the whole list.

demonstrates swap-the-layersall-or-nothingempty-succeedstyped-signature
3 hints
  1. The return type has one Maybe on the outside, so there is one decision to make, not one per element.
  2. A single none anywhere settles it. Everything after that is irrelevant.
  3. An empty array had no failure in it, so read the return type again and ask what that means.
Solution
type Maybe<A> =
  | { tag: 'some', value: A }
  | { tag: 'none' }

const some = <A>(value: A): Maybe<A> => ({ tag: 'some', value })
const none: Maybe<never> = { tag: 'none' }

const sequence = <A>(xs: Maybe<A>[]): Maybe<A[]> => {
  const out: A[] = []
  for (const m of xs) {
    if (m.tag === 'none') return none
    out.push(m.value)
  }
  return some(out)
}

  | { tag: 'none' }

type Maybe<A> =
  | { tag: 'some', value: A }
  | { tag: 'none' }

const some = <A>(value: A): Maybe<A> => ({ tag: 'some', value })
const none: Maybe<never> = { tag: 'none' }

const sequence = <A>(xs: Maybe<A>[]): Maybe<A[]> => {
  if (xs.length === 0) return none
  const out: A[] = []
  for (const m of xs) {
    if (m.tag === 'none') return none
    out.push(m.value)
  }
  return some(out)
}
type Maybe<A> =
  | { tag: 'some', value: A }
  | { tag: 'none' }

const some = <A>(value: A): Maybe<A> => ({ tag: 'some', value })
const none: Maybe<never> = { tag: 'none' }

const sequence = <A>(xs: Maybe<A>[]): Maybe<A[]> => {
  const out: A[] = []
  for (const m of xs) {
    if (m.tag === 'none') return none
    out.unshift(m.value)
  }
  return some(out)
}

Morphism

Category & Morphisms 2 rubric 2 rungs 2 variants
  1. 2 Can identify the objects and the arrow in a piece of code, and knows the arrow need not be reversible.
  2. 1 Can name a morphism by the relationship it has to its endpoints: endo, iso, or neither.

Rungs

Objects and arrows

choicerecognize

In const length = (s: String): Number => s.length, which statements are true?

demonstrates objects-and-arrows
  1. correct
    // String and Number are the objects

    The things the arrow goes between.

  2. correct
    // length is the morphism

    The arrow itself: a transformation from one object to another.

  3. wrong
    // The morphism has to be reversible

    Only an isomorphism is. Many strings share a length, so this one cannot be undone.

  4. correct
    // A morphism can go from an object to itself

    That is an endomorphism, and it is perfectly ordinary.

Classify the arrows

codeimplement

Write classify, which reports how a morphism relates its two objects: endo when they are the same, and iso when a reverse morphism is supplied.

demonstrates the-named-kindsobjects-and-arrows
2 hints
  1. The names describe the shape of the arrow, not what it computes.
  2. A morphism from an object to itself that also has an inverse is both, and "auto" is the word for it.
Solution
// classify :: ({ from, to, hasInverse }) -> String
const classify = (arrow) => {
  const endo = arrow.from === arrow.to
  const iso = arrow.hasInverse === true
  if (endo && iso) return 'auto'
  if (endo) return 'endo'
  if (iso) return 'iso'
  return 'plain'
}
Starter · 1 export: classify

// "endo"  from and to are the same object
// "iso"   there is an inverse
// "auto"  both
// "plain" neither

2 variants that must fail

  if (arrow.hasInverse === true) return 'iso'
  return 'plain'
}

Semigroupoid

Category & Morphisms 3 rubric 2 rungs 2 variants
  1. 1 Can write a compose that returns the same wrapper, so three morphisms chain.
  2. 1 Knows compose reads right to left, so the argument runs first.
  3. 2 Knows associativity is the only requirement, and that an identity is what a category adds.

Rungs

Composition that associates

codeimplement

A Semigroupoid is anything with an associative compose. Wrap a function so composing two of them gives another one.

demonstrates compose-stays-insidedirectionassociativity-only
2 hints
  1. compose reads right to left: a.compose(b) runs b first.
  2. The result has to be another wrapper, or a chain of three will not work.
Solution
// Morphism :: (a -> b) -> Semigroupoid a b
const Morphism = (f) => ({
  f,
  compose: (other) => Morphism((x) => f(other.f(x))),
  run: (x) => f(x),
  inspect: () => 'Morphism(?)'
})

  },
  run: (x) => f(x),
  inspect: () => 'Morphism(?)'

  f,

Semigroupoid or category?

choicerecognize

What does a Category have that a Semigroupoid does not?

demonstrates associativity-only
  1. correct
    // An identity morphism, so composing with it changes nothing

    Exactly the same step as semigroup to monoid: add the neutral element.

  2. wrong
    // Associative composition

    A semigroupoid already has that. It is the only thing it has.

  3. wrong
    // A way to map over the contents

    Neither has one. That is Functor.

  4. wrong
    // Composition that works in both directions

    Neither offers that. Reversing an arrow needs an isomorphism.

Category

Category & Morphisms 3 rubric 2 rungs 2 variants
  1. 1 Can supply an identity morphism and knows it must be neutral composed on either side.
  2. 1 Knows the identity has to be a morphism itself, not a bare function, so it can be composed.
  3. 1 Can say what a category requires and what it does not, in particular that arrows need no inverses. recognition only

Rungs

Composition with an identity

codeimplement

A Category is a Semigroupoid with an id that leaves any morphism alone, composed on either side.

demonstrates identity-morphismid-is-a-morphism
2 hints
  1. id is the morphism that does nothing at all to its input.
  2. It hangs off the constructor: Morphism.id().
Solution
const Morphism = (f) => ({
  f,
  compose: (other) => Morphism((x) => f(other.f(x))),
  run: (x) => f(x),
  inspect: () => 'Morphism(?)'
})

// id :: () -> Category a a
Morphism.id = () => Morphism((x) => x)

What makes a category?

choicerecognize

Select every requirement a Category has.

demonstrates what-is-required
  1. correct
    // Composition is associative

    Inherited from Semigroupoid.

  2. correct
    // There is an identity for every object

    And it has to be neutral composed on either side.

  3. wrong
    // Every morphism has an inverse

    That would be a groupoid. Most categories have arrows you cannot reverse.

  4. wrong
    // The objects are types and the morphisms are functions

    One example, not a requirement. Objects and arrows can be many things.

Endomorphism

Category & Morphisms 3 rubric 2 rungs 2 variants
  1. 1 Can recognize an endomorphism from its signature, and knows why the same type at both ends matters. recognition only
  2. 1 Knows endomorphisms form a monoid under composition, with identity as the empty.
  3. 1 Can combine a list of transformations into one, using the monoid, including the empty case.

Rungs

Which of these are endomorphisms?

choicerecognize

An endomorphism takes a type and gives back the same type. Select every one.

demonstrates same-type-both-ends
  1. correct
    // uppercase :: String -> String

    In and out are both String.

  2. wrong
    // length :: String -> Number

    Different type out, so not an endomorphism.

  3. correct
    // negate :: Number -> Number

    Number to Number.

  4. correct
    // sort :: [a] -> [a]

    A list in, a list of the same element type out.

  5. wrong
    // head :: [a] -> a

    A list goes in and an element comes out, which are different types.

Endomorphisms form a monoid

codeimplement

Because an endomorphism returns what it was given, two of them always compose. That makes them a monoid, with identity for its empty.

demonstrates monoid-under-compositionfold-a-pipeline
2 hints
  1. concat composes the two functions. Pick an order and be consistent.
  2. The empty element is the function that changes nothing.
Solution
// Endo :: (a -> a) -> Endo a
const Endo = (run) => ({
  run,
  // Left to right: this one, then the other.
  concat: (other) => Endo((x) => other.run(run(x))),
  inspect: () => 'Endo(?)'
})

// empty :: () -> Endo a
Endo.empty = () => Endo((x) => x)

}

  run,
  concat: (other) => Endo(run),
  inspect: () => 'Endo(?)'
})
Endo.empty = () => Endo((x) => x)

Isomorphism

Category & Morphisms 3 rubric 2 rungs 2 variants
  1. 1 Can write a pair of morphisms that undo each other in both directions.
  2. 1 Knows one round trip holding is not enough, and can name a pair that satisfies only one. recognition only
  3. 1 Knows two isomorphic types carry the same information, so a function on one transfers to the other.

Rungs

A pair of morphisms that undo each other

codeimplement

An isomorphism is two arrows whose composition is identity in both directions. Write the pair between a coordinate record and a pair.

demonstrates pair-of-arrowssame-information
1 hint
  1. Neither direction may lose or invent anything. Both fields have to survive the trip.
Solution
// pairToCoords :: [Number, Number] -> { x, y }
const pairToCoords = (pair) => ({ x: pair[0], y: pair[1] })

// coordsToPair :: { x, y } -> [Number, Number]
const coordsToPair = (coords) => [coords.x, coords.y]

What makes it an isomorphism rather than two functions?

choicerecognize

Two conversions between the same pair of types. What has to be true for them to be an isomorphism?

demonstrates both-ways
  1. correct
    // from(to(x)) === x  and  to(from(y)) === y

    Both round trips, not just one. A one-way round trip is a retraction, not an isomorphism.

  2. wrong
    // from(to(x)) === x  is enough

    Only half. Number to String satisfies one direction and still loses "007".

  3. wrong
    // The two types have the same field names

    Irrelevant. A pair and a record share no names and are perfectly isomorphic.

  4. wrong
    // Both conversions are pure

    Necessary but nowhere near sufficient. Math.round is pure and loses information.

Homomorphism

Category & Morphisms 3 rubric 2 rungs 3 variants
  1. 2 Knows a homomorphism makes combining before the map equal combining after, and can state that as an equation.
  2. 1 Can tell whether a map between two combining operations preserves the structure, including cases where it does not. recognition only
  3. 1 Knows the identity of the source has to map to the identity of the target.

Rungs

A map that preserves the structure

codeimplement

A homomorphism carries one structure into another so that combining before or after the map gives the same answer. Write one from lists to their lengths.

demonstrates structure-preservingidentity-maps-to-identity
2 hints
  1. Lists combine by concatenation; numbers combine by addition.
  2. length(a ++ b) has to equal length(a) + length(b). That equation is the whole definition.
Solution
// toLength :: [a] -> Number
const toLength = (xs) => xs.length

// combineLists :: ([a], [a]) -> [a]
const combineLists = (a, b) => [...a, ...b]

// combineLengths :: (Number, Number) -> Number
const combineLengths = (a, b) => a + b

Which of these are homomorphisms?

choicerecognize

Each maps one monoid into another. Select the ones where combining before the map equals combining after.

demonstrates identifystructure-preserving
  1. correct
    // length :: ([a], ++) -> (Number, +)

    The length of a join is the sum of the lengths.

  2. correct
    // sum :: ([Number], ++) -> (Number, +)

    The sum of a joined list is the sum of the two sums.

  3. correct
    // head :: ([a], ++) -> (a, first)

    The head of a join is the head of the first, which is what "keep the first" does. It needs a Maybe for the empty case.

  4. wrong
    // maximum :: ([Number], ++) -> (Number, +)

    The maximum of a join is the larger of the two maxima, not their sum. It would be a homomorphism into (Number, max).

  5. wrong
    // unique :: ([a], ++) -> (Number, +)

    Joining two lists that share an element loses one from the count, so the two sides disagree.

Natural Transformation

Category & Morphisms 4 rubric 3 rungs 6 variants typed lap
  1. 2 Can convert one functor into another without touching the values inside.
  2. 3 Can state the naturality law and say what it forbids: looking at the values on the way through.
  3. 1 Knows it may change how many elements there are, as head does, and still be natural.
  4. 1 Can say why binding A on the function rather than on the type is what stops a natural transformation from looking at the elements.

Lap 1 · JavaScript

Change the container, not the contents

codeimplement

A natural transformation moves a value from one functor to another without touching what is inside. Write head :: Array a -> Maybe a.

demonstrates changes-the-containernaturalitymay-drop-elements
2 hints
  1. An empty array has no head, so that case is Nothing.
  2. Whatever you do, do not apply a function to the element on the way through. Naturality is exactly the promise that you did not.
Solution
const Just = (value) => ({ isNothing: false, value, map: (f) => Just(f(value)) })
const Nothing = () => ({ isNothing: true, map: () => Nothing() })

// head :: Array a -> Maybe a
const head = (xs) => (xs.length > 0 ? Just(xs[0]) : Nothing())

What does naturality forbid?

choicerecognize

The law says nat(fa.map(f)) equals nat(fa).map(f). What does that rule out?

demonstrates naturality
  1. correct
    // The transformation looking at the values it carries

    If it inspected or changed them, mapping before and after would give different answers. It can only work on the shape.

  2. wrong
    // The transformation changing the number of elements

    It can. head drops all but one, and that is perfectly natural.

  3. wrong
    // The transformation being partial

    head is partial in spirit, which is why it returns a Maybe. Naturality is untroubled.

  4. wrong
    // Using map inside the transformation

    Not forbidden in itself. Mapping with identity would be fine, if pointless.

Lap 2 · With types

Satisfy ArrayToMaybe

codeimplementlap 2 · ts

The alias is given. Write head so it changes the container without ever consulting what is inside it.

demonstrates changes-the-containernaturalitytyped-signature
3 hints
  1. A is bound on the function, so the body has to work for every type at once.
  2. Ask about the array, not about the elements. xs.length is fair game; xs[0] > 0 is not.
  3. An empty array has no first element, and there is no value of type A you could invent instead.
Solution
type Maybe<A> =
  | { tag: 'some', value: A }
  | { tag: 'none' }

const some = <A>(value: A): Maybe<A> => ({ tag: 'some', value })
const none: Maybe<never> = { tag: 'none' }

type ArrayToMaybe = <A>(xs: A[]) => Maybe<A>

const head: ArrayToMaybe = (xs) => (xs.length ? some(xs[0]) : none)

type ArrayToMaybe = <A>(xs: A[]) => Maybe<A>

const head: ArrayToMaybe = (xs) => {
  const hit = (xs as unknown[]).find((x) => x)
  return hit === undefined ? none : some(hit as never)
}

Catamorphism

Category & Morphisms 3 rubric 2 rungs 3 variants
  1. 1 Can write a fold and express several operations in terms of it, differing only in the step and the seed.
  2. 1 Knows the seed decides the empty case and must be the identity for the operation.
  3. 1 Knows cata tears down where ana builds up, and that neither is tied to lists. recognition only

Rungs

Collapse a structure to a value

codeimplement

A catamorphism tears a structure down to one value. Write cata once, then express sum, max, and length in terms of it.

demonstrates fold-to-a-valueseed-is-the-identity
2 hints
  1. cata is a fold: an accumulator, a step, and a list.
  2. Each of the three is the same fold with a different step and seed.
Solution
// cata :: ((b, a) -> b) -> b -> [a] -> b
const cata = (step) => (seed) => (xs) => {
  let acc = seed
  for (const x of xs) acc = step(acc, x)
  return acc
}

const sum = cata((a, b) => a + b)(0)
const max = cata((a, b) => (b > a ? b : a))(-Infinity)
const length = cata((a) => a + 1)(0)

Which direction?

choicerecognize

A catamorphism and an anamorphism are opposites. Which is which?

demonstrates direction
  1. correct
    // cata tears a structure down to a value;
// ana builds a structure up from a value

    Cata is a fold, ana is an unfold. The Greek is "downwards" and "upwards".

  2. wrong
    // cata builds up; ana tears down

    The right idea, the wrong way round.

  3. wrong
    // Both tear down, but cata is recursive

    Both are recursive. It is the direction that differs.

  4. wrong
    // cata works on lists; ana works on trees

    Neither is tied to a shape. Both are defined for any recursive structure.

Anamorphism

Category & Morphisms 3 rubric 2 rungs 5 variants
  1. 1 Can write an unfold whose step either produces the next value and seed, or signals the end.
  2. 2 Knows the step is what stops it, and can get an inclusive or exclusive boundary right.
  3. 1 Can express a generator of a sequence as an unfold rather than a hand-written loop.

Rungs

Build a structure from a seed

codeimplement

An anamorphism is an unfold: the opposite of a fold. Write unfold, then use it for range and countDown.

demonstrates build-from-a-seedtermination
2 hints
  1. The step returns either the next value and the next seed, or nothing to stop.
  2. [value, nextSeed] to continue, null to finish.
Solution
// unfold :: ((b -> [a, b] | null), b) -> [a]
const unfold = (step, seed) => {
  const out = []
  let current = seed
  let next = step(current)
  while (next !== null) {
    out.push(next[0])
    current = next[1]
    next = step(current)
  }
  return out
}

// range :: (Number, Number) -> [Number]
const range = (from, to) => unfold((n) => (n < to ? [n, n + 1] : null), from)

// countDown :: Number -> [Number]
const countDown = (n) => unfold((k) => (k > 0 ? [k, k - 1] : null), n)

Unfold the digits of a number

codeapply

Use the same unfold to write digits, which gives the decimal digits of a number, most significant first.

demonstrates use-ittermination
2 hints
  1. Peeling from the right is easier: take n % 10 and continue with Math.floor(n / 10).
  2. Then put them the right way round.
Solution
const unfold = (step, seed) => {
  const out = []
  let current = seed
  let next = step(current)
  while (next !== null) {
    out.push(next[0])
    current = next[1]
    next = step(current)
  }
  return out
}

// digits :: Number -> [Number]
const digits = (n) => {
  if (n === 0) return [0]
  return unfold((k) => (k > 0 ? [k % 10, Math.floor(k / 10)] : null), n).reverse()
}

  return out
}
const digits = (n) => {
  if (n === 0) return [0]
  return unfold((k) => (k > 0 ? [k % 10, Math.floor(k / 10)] : null), n)
}
const unfold = (step, seed) => {
  const out = []
  let current = seed
  let next = step(current)
  while (next !== null) {
    out.push(next[0]); current = next[1]; next = step(current)
  }
  return out
}
const digits = (n) => unfold((k) => (k > 0 ? [k % 10, Math.floor(k / 10)] : null), n).reverse()

Hylomorphism

Category & Morphisms 3 rubric 2 rungs 3 variants
  1. 1 Can compose an unfold and a fold into one function over a seed.
  2. 1 Knows the structure in the middle exists only to be consumed, which is what makes fusing the two halves possible. recognition only
  3. 1 Knows the shape is not specific to any one problem, and can use the same hylo for a different fold.

Rungs

Unfold then fold

codeapply

A hylomorphism builds a structure and immediately tears it down. Compose the two you already have, and use the result for factorial.

demonstrates unfold-then-foldgeneral
2 hints
  1. hylo(fold, unfold) unfolds the seed into a list, then folds that list.
  2. Factorial is: unfold n down to 1, then fold by multiplying.
Solution
const unfold = (step, seed) => {
  const out = []
  let current = seed
  let next = step(current)
  while (next !== null) {
    out.push(next[0]); current = next[1]; next = step(current)
  }
  return out
}
const cata = (step, seed, xs) => xs.reduce(step, seed)

// hylo :: ((b, a) -> b, b, (c -> [a, c] | null)) -> c -> b
const hylo = (foldStep, foldSeed, unfoldStep) => (seed) =>
  cata(foldStep, foldSeed, unfold(unfoldStep, seed))

// factorial :: Number -> Number
const factorial = hylo(
  (acc, n) => acc * n,
  1,
  (n) => (n > 0 ? [n, n - 1] : null)
)

Why name it at all?

choicerecognize

A hylomorphism is just an unfold followed by a fold. What is the point of the name?

demonstrates intermediate-is-throwaway
  1. correct
    // The intermediate structure is never needed,
// so the two halves can be fused into one pass

    Recognizing the shape is what lets a compiler, or you, remove the list that only ever existed to be consumed.

  2. wrong
    // It is faster than either half alone

    It does strictly more work than either half. The saving is in fusing them.

  3. wrong
    // It is the only way to write a recursive function

    Plain recursion works fine. This is about naming a recurring shape.

  4. wrong
    // It guarantees the recursion terminates

    It does not. An unfold that never returns null runs forever.

Paramorphism

Category & Morphisms 3 rubric 2 rungs 3 variants
  1. 2 Knows a paramorphism gives the step the unconsumed remainder as well as the accumulator and the element.
  2. 1 Knows the remainder is what comes after the current element, not including it.
  3. 1 Can express something that needs the remainder, such as the suffixes of a list, which an ordinary fold cannot.

Rungs

A fold that can see what is left

codeimplement

A catamorphism only sees the accumulator and one element. A paramorphism also sees the rest of the structure. Write para, then use it for suffixes.

demonstrates sees-the-remainderexcludes-currentuse-it
2 hints
  1. The step takes three things: the accumulator, the head, and the remaining tail.
  2. suffixes is the classic example, because each step needs the tail it has not folded yet.
Solution
// para :: ((b, a, [a]) -> b) -> b -> [a] -> b
const para = (step) => (seed) => (xs) => {
  let acc = seed
  for (let i = 0; i < xs.length; i++) {
    acc = step(acc, xs[i], xs.slice(i + 1))
  }
  return acc
}

// suffixes :: [a] -> [[a]]
const suffixes = para((acc, head, tail) => [...acc, tail])([])

What does para add?

choicerecognize

Compared with an ordinary fold, what extra does a paramorphism give the step function?

demonstrates sees-the-remainder
  1. correct
    // The part of the structure not yet folded

    That is the whole difference, and it is what makes suffixes expressible.

  2. wrong
    // The index of the current element

    Useful, and easy to thread through an ordinary fold. Not what para is for.

  3. wrong
    // The ability to stop early

    Neither offers that on its own.

  4. wrong
    // The result of folding the rest

    Close, but it is the unfolded remainder, not its result. Getting the result would be circular.

Apomorphism

Category & Morphisms 3 rubric 2 rungs 3 variants
  1. 2 Knows an apomorphism can stop generating and hand back the whole remainder in one step.
  2. 1 Can write a step that continues, finishes with a whole structure, or stops with nothing.
  3. 1 Can express something that stops early and keeps the rest untouched, such as inserting into a sorted list.

Rungs

An unfold that can finish early

codeimplement

An apomorphism is an unfold that can stop generating and hand back the rest in one go. Write apo, then use it to insert a value into a sorted list.

demonstrates early-finishthree-outcomesuse-it
2 hints
  1. The step returns one of three things: continue with a seed, finish with a whole list, or stop.
  2. Once the insertion point is found, the rest of the list can be returned as it is.
Solution
// apo :: ((b -> Step), b) -> [a]
const apo = (step, seed) => {
  const out = []
  let current = seed
  while (true) {
    const result = step(current)
    if (result === null) return out
    if (result.done !== undefined) return out.concat(result.done)
    out.push(result.next[0])
    current = result.next[1]
  }
}

// insert :: (Number, [Number]) -> [Number]
const insert = (x, sorted) =>
  apo((rest) => {
    if (rest.length === 0) return { done: [x] }
    if (x <= rest[0]) return { done: [x, ...rest] }
    return { next: [rest[0], rest.slice(1)] }
  }, sorted)

//   null                     stop with nothing more

const insert = (x, sorted) => {
  const out = []
  let placed = false
  for (const v of sorted) {
    if (!placed && x <= v) { out.push(x); placed = true }
    out.push(v)
  }
  if (!placed) out.push(x)
  return out
}

    if (rest.length === 0) return { done: [x] }
    if (x <= rest[0]) return { done: rest }
    return { next: [rest[0], rest.slice(1)] }

Which unfold is which?

choicerecognize

An anamorphism and an apomorphism both build a structure. What can the apomorphism do that the anamorphism cannot?

demonstrates early-finish
  1. correct
    // Stop early and hand back the rest of the structure in one step

    An ordinary unfold can only produce one element at a time, or stop. An apomorphism can also say "and the remainder is this".

  2. wrong
    // Produce an infinite structure

    An ordinary unfold does that perfectly well by never stopping.

  3. wrong
    // See the part it has already produced

    Neither can. Looking at what is left is the paramorphism, and that is a fold.

  4. wrong
    // Run in constant memory

    Unrelated to the distinction. Both build as much structure as they are asked for.
